16 Trajectory Fine-Tuning

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why can training on a useful recovery record teach the model to repeat the mistake instead of the correction?

While prompt engineering and runtime guardrails guide an off-the-shelf model, fine-tuning modifies the neural network weights directly, embedding specialized decision policies and tool calling conventions into model parameters. However, training on multi-turn trajectories requires strict separation between agent proposals and external environment observations, so that imitation loss applies only to selected policy responses while environment feedback remains conditioning context. Naive fine-tuning can teach a policy to fabricate its own tool results or overfit to static workspace layouts, degrading general reasoning and error recovery. Systems engineering governs how datasets are partitioned, how loss masks are applied, and how updated checkpoints are validated against regression suites before deployment. An update is worth releasing only when closed-loop task acceptance improves under the same runtime that still bounds every proposal the model makes, however far its training loss has fallen.

Learning Objectives
  • Assign agent behavior to weights, context, or runtime, and decide when a failure warrants a weight change
  • Construct model inputs that preserve intended conditioning and price per-invocation serialization in training tokens
  • Map admitted response spans to causal token targets, observation masks, and termination policy
  • Compare loss weighting and construct batches without unintended cross-example dependencies
  • Calculate imitation regret bounds on reference and learner states to decide when on-policy labeling pays
  • Calculate preference margins and gradient weights, and distinguish what a comparison measures from task acceptance
  • Select behavior sources and parameter artifacts using explicit resource and version accounting
  • Evaluate a versioned policy for release on closed-loop acceptance, cost per accepted task, and schema transfer, while preserving runtime enforcement
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

16.1 Recovery Records as Training Data

Fine-tuning reshapes each model call but leaves every exposure to the runtime.

Consider a recovery demonstration admitted by the curation pipeline for the repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) into repair_demonstrations_v1. During the recorded session, the agent generated a malformed edit proposal that the runtime validator refused prior to tool dispatch. In response to the failure notification, the model inspected the tool schema, generated a corrected edit, and produced candidate patch ws_r20. From this multi-turn trajectory, the target-selection policy recovery_targets_v1 marks three assistant turns as supervised targets: the schema inspection request (e3), the corrected edit (e4), and the scoped final summary (e6). The malformed proposal (e2) and the runtime’s refusal remain in the input history as conditioning context. The member that entered the corpus is the redacted view trace_r19_01_redacted_v1, and the corpus manifest binds it to those role assignments, its lineage, and its split membership.

The raw event log of an admitted record is not yet in a form a training loop can safely ingest, because it interleaves model generations, runtime observations, and tool responses in the order they occurred. If a data loader treats every assistant message as a supervised target, it computes loss on the malformed edit proposal alongside the correction and reinforces the initial mistake. If the training pipeline computes loss across all sequence tokens indiscriminately, the model spends parameter capacity predicting deterministic environment replies, such as compiler diagnostics or directory listings, that it will never emit during inference. Finally, if the batching pipeline packs multiple trajectories into a shared context window without attention masking, self-attention heads attend across sample boundaries and leak private workspace contents into unrelated training examples. Standard cross-entropy loss fails silently under each of these mistakes. The training loss falls steadily as the optimizer fits the data, hiding the corrupted behavior from offline metrics. While curation contracts decide which actions show desirable behavior and evaluation contracts measure the deployed agent, neither specifies how to turn a multi-turn execution trace into isolated, causal gradient updates.

The path from an admitted record to an evaluated policy update begins with a question curation leaves open, namely which parts of the repair behavior belong in weights at all (section 16.2). The answer fixes what the data pipeline must protect. Serialization reconstructs each model input exactly as the agent received it (section 16.3), loss masking places the gradient on the selected responses and nowhere else (section 16.4), and batching shares buffers without letting examples read one another or skew one another’s weight (section 16.5). Masking and batching guard directly against those three silent failures, and serialization guards the premise both rely on, that each prefix is the one the model actually saw.

A faithful loader still trains on recorded prefixes, and the deployed policy acts on prefixes its own choices produce. Exposure bias prices that gap over the horizon and decides when labeling the learner’s own states pays (section 16.6), and schema robustness asks the same question of the tool interfaces the policy is shown (section 16.7). The remaining sections vary the update itself along three independent choices. The first is the objective, where preference optimization contrasts valid alternatives that imitation cannot rank (section 16.8). The second is the behavior source, where distillation moves a costlier configuration’s behavior into a cheaper one (section 16.9). The last is the parameter artifact, where adapters change what a run must store (section 16.10). Closed-loop evaluation decides whether any of them earned a release (section 16.11), and a training manifest for the repair policy assembles the whole chain (section 16.12).

16.2 Scope of Supervised Fine-Tuning

Before turning an admitted record such as trace_r19_01_redacted_v1 into a weight update, engineers must decide whether the failure it repaired belongs in the model’s parameters at all. Fine-tuning a model to work around a missing prompt instruction or a broken tool schema bakes a temporary workaround into billions of weights, only for the behavior to break again the next time the interface changes. Modifying model weights is an expensive, high-blast-radius change reserved for behavioral failures that survive cheaper fixes. The intervention ladder orders changes by cost and reversibility, so engineers test prompt, schema, and runtime fixes before spending compute on parameter updates, while diagnostic evidence can justify a direct weight update when the policy itself is at fault. Verified trajectory post-training (principle 13) requires curation to tie recorded evidence to a declared learning use and separate admissible targets from contextual or rejected actions. While this ordering is the default because of cost and blast radius, fine-tuning also pays when moving stable procedural guidance into the weights costs less than resending a long instructional prefix on every turn (section 16.9).

↩︎ The intervention ladder, which orders context, schema, runtime, and parameter interventions by cost and reversibility, is established in The intervention ladder.

Once diagnostic evidence justifies a parameter update, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) adjusts trainable model parameters to maximize the conditional likelihood of selected target tokens given their causal prefix context. When processing trace_r19_01_redacted_v1, the training loop must follow the recovery sequence defined in recovery_targets_v1 rather than computing loss over every recorded token. The model must see the historical context and tool outputs that justify a chosen action, yet the data pipeline must exclude later steps that would reveal future observations or solutions ahead of time. Trajectory fine-tuning therefore inherits two data contracts: a target-selection contract that designates which tokens contribute to the loss gradient, and a conditioning contract that governs what causal history the model sees when generating those tokens.

The two contracts separate learning improved behavior from overfitting an idiosyncratic run. In an error-recovery trajectory, the behavioral goal is teaching the model to inspect an updated schema after a refusal, issue a properly formed corrective call, and interpret what the runtime verification check establishes. Memorizing the file paths of a specific test fixture or predicting hard-coded test outputs lowers training loss without teaching generalizable recovery skills. Whether the updated policy transfers these habits to unfamiliar environments and new tasks is what section 16.11 measures, and keeping the update generalizable starts with deciding which parts of agent behavior live in weights versus the prompt and runtime.

16.2.1 Procedure, contracts, and enforcement

If a fine-tuned repair agent memorizes an API signature in its parameters instead of reading the prompt, a routine tool-interface change, such as adding a required parameter or revoking a permission, immediately breaks the deployed policy. Every response the agent generates combines the model’s static weights with the dynamic context window assembled for that call, and fine-tuning updates only the weights. Engineers therefore divide agent behavior across three architectural homes, summarized in table 1:

  • Procedure is behavior that stays stable across tasks and changes slowly, such as inspecting diagnostic output before modifying source code or checking unresolved issues before reporting completion. Procedure is what model weights can reliably absorb.
  • Contracts are the dynamic operational facts that vary across deployments, user sessions, and individual turns, such as API tool definitions, schema requirements, environment parameters, and granted permissions. Contracts belong in the prompt context; a model that memorized an earlier schema will keep emitting obsolete arguments when an interface changes.
  • Enforcement covers the invariants that must hold even when the model proposes an invalid or adversarial action, such as schema validation, authorization checks, sandbox containment, and execution timeouts. Enforcement stays in the deterministic runtime harness under invariant closure (principle 1).

Relying on parameters to store interface contracts makes a deployment brittle, because a revoked permission stays revoked regardless of how often the action appeared in past training demonstrations. Fine-tuning cannot replace dynamic contracts; what it can teach is the habit of reading and following whichever contract the context window supplies.

This boundary disproves the view that fine-tuning only moves the prompt into the weights. Only the procedural parts of a prompt (structured reasoning guidelines, diagnostic checklists, or few-shot error-recovery examples) belong in the parameters, where they save token overhead on every turn (section 16.9). The contractual parts must stay in the prompt context, because omitting them forces the model to guess the current execution environment. The deterministic checks that enforce authorization and boundary invariants cannot live in either prompts or weights, because a probability distribution over tokens cannot guarantee an invariant.

Fine-tuning changes the policy’s proposal distribution: which tools it requests, how thoroughly it inspects diagnostic evidence, and which execution paths it tries. Those shifts change task success rates, end-to-end latency, and token use, yet every proposed action still runs inside the horizons, state transitions, and authority boundaries enforced by the runtime harness. This separation is the proposal boundary of The Model Call Boundary. Training reshapes what the policy proposes, while the runtime decides which actions may execute, and that boundary governs every adaptation choice that follows.

16.2.2 Objective, behavior source, and artifact

Deciding that a recovery habit belongs in the weights still leaves a team to choose whether to imitate single corrections or rank competing repairs, whether to train on recorded logs or collect fresh trajectories, and whether to ship a full checkpoint copy or a lightweight adapter. Treating “fine-tuning” as a single knob obscures which decision is responsible when an update overfits or exceeds its memory budget, so the pipeline separates adaptation along three independent axes:

  • Training Objective: The loss function that defines the learning signal. Supervised imitation maximizes the likelihood of curated target demonstrations, whereas preference methods such as direct preference optimization (section 16.8) adjust weights by contrasting preferred trajectories against dispreferred alternatives under an explicit ranking rule.
  • Behavior Source: Where the demonstrations and conditioning inputs come from. Traces may be collected from instrumented production runs and filtered by offline verification, generated by a teacher configuration with privileged context or a larger search budget (section 16.9), labeled on the states the learner itself reaches, or constructed by injecting faults the recordings never contained (section 16.6.2).
  • Parameter Artifact: Which weights the run updates and stores. Teams can update all model parameters directly or train compact adapter matrices, such as low-rank adaptation (LoRA) modules, that attach to a frozen base checkpoint (section 16.10).

Because these three choices are independent, they combine freely; a deployment can, for example, train a low-rank adapter with a preference objective on synthetic teacher demonstrations. Whichever combination a team chooses, the run still trains on trajectory records converted into model inputs, so the first pipeline step is reconstructing the input prefix and conditioning window for each selected response.

16.3 Serializing Trajectories

In the admitted repair demonstration trace_r19_01_redacted_v1, the corrected edit (e4) makes sense only as a response to the malformed proposal (e2), the runtime refusal, and the schema inspection (e3) that preceded it. A training loader that strips out the refusal changes the problem the subsequent correction answered, and a loader that places the later passing test check (e5) before the edit leaks future evidence that the model did not have when proposing the patch. While curation selects which model completions to reinforce, serialization must reconstruct the exact prompt and observation history that prompted them, including any runtime redactions (such as synthetic placeholders substituted for private file paths), so the training example matches the context the model actually saw.

16.3.1 Reconstructing each model input

Runtime event logs record state transitions across the harness, but an event log alone does not show what was inside the model’s context window at a given call. System prompts change, tool schemas update between versions, and earlier messages are often summarized or truncated to fit token budgets. To reconstruct each call’s input faithfully, the training pipeline reads the delivered-input manifests, which record the exact messages, tool definitions, summaries, and bounded observations sent to the model along with their schema versions.

↩︎ The delivered-input manifest that records the exact payload sent to the model, rather than mutable paths or references, is established in The log event schema.

When an example requires historical fidelity, the loader serializes the selected invocation directly from its delivered-input manifest, preserving partial buffers, truncated outputs, and execution errors rather than substituting a cleaner result obtained later in the run. When a pipeline instead transforms an example deliberately, such as shortening prompts for context distillation (section 16.9), it applies the reviewed transformation to both the prompt and the paired response, keeps the parent trace reference, and rechecks schema compatibility. Table 2 shows how the curated responses in the running repair demonstration map to their conditioning evidence; in the rendered training example, each row also carries the system instructions and active tool definitions delivered at that turn.

The refusal at e2 shows that runtime validation caught the malformed proposal before dispatch, so no tool ran on the host. Because the check summary at e5 arrived only after the agent issued the corrected edit, it can condition the final report at e6 but cannot appear in the prefix for e4. Selecting the schema request at e3 likewise trains the model’s query tokens while leaving the returned schema as an external observation, so that loss masking in the next stage can separate model decisions from environment replies.

16.3.2 Rendering with versioned templates

Once the delivered messages for e3, e4, and e6 are reconstructed, the loader must turn those structured message objects (system instructions, user prompts, assistant reasoning, tool calls, and environment returns) into the exact token sequence the model expects at serving time. Chat templates differ across model families, and even checkpoints from the same base model often format tool schemas, parallel calls, or message boundaries differently. Rendering a trajectory with delimiters that do not match the serving parser teaches the policy a syntax that production will reject. A serialized dataset artifact therefore records the tokenizer version, template configuration, tool-formatting schema, and response-boundary policy alongside the token arrays, because the message sequence and its rendering configuration together form the model input.

In schematic form, a multi-turn tool-calling example looks like the sequence below. The delimiter strings are illustrative placeholders, since each model family defines its own special tokens. The system segment gives the task contract and tool definitions. The assistant segment emits a reasoning trace followed by two parallel tool calls with unique identifiers. Each tool segment returns an observation bound to its call identifier.

<|system|> task contract; schemas for read_file(target_uri), run_tests(target) <|end|>
<|user|> Fix the connection-pool leak in src/pool.py <|end|>
<|assistant|> <|reasoning|> Reproduce the failure, then read the module. <|end_reasoning|>
  <|call id=c1|> {"name": "read_file", "arguments": {"target_uri": "src/pool.py"}} <|end_call|>
  <|call id=c2|> {"name": "run_tests", "arguments": {"target": "tests/test_pool.py"}} <|end_call|>
<|end_turn|>
<|tool id=c2|> FAILED test_release: pool exhausted <|end|>
<|tool id=c1|> ...file contents... <|end|>
<|assistant|> ...

In this sequence, c2 finishes before c1, and because the runtime delivers observations in arrival order, the listing places the c2 reply first to match the prompt the model actually received on its next turn. The call identifiers keep each observation bound to its original call regardless of arrival order. Re-sorting the replies into call order (c1, then c2) for tidiness would invent a prompt the model never saw, and reordering calls whose arguments or observations depend on one another changes the causal problem into a synthetic transformation that needs its own validation. The <|end_turn|> token marks where the policy handed control back to the runtime loop, while all tokens between <|tool|> and <|end|> are external observations.

The training pipeline packages this rendered dataset as serialization_v1, whose manifest pairs the admitted execution records with the active rendering configuration and maps every selected response span to its token offsets. Because tokenization, template formatting, and string redaction can shift token boundaries (for example, when leading whitespace merges into an adjacent special token), the pipeline re-verifies all token offsets against the rendered token array and decodes completed examples to check task prompts, tool bindings, span boundaries, and end-of-sequence markers. If an example fails boundary alignment or drops content, the pipeline discards the record rather than patching it with synthetic filler.

Role delimiters define protocol syntax, but they carry no runtime authority: if a model emits text that mimics a <|tool|> delimiter, that text is still model output under the tokenizer’s grammar, and the serialization pipeline never promotes unverified generations into environment observations. Once the template fixes how each turn is rendered, the pipeline still has to decide whether a multi-turn trajectory can be stored as a single rendered sequence or must be split into separate per-invocation examples.

↩︎ The rule that model output is a proposal carrying no authority until the runtime validates it is established in The Model Call Boundary.

16.3.3 One example per invocation

Because the repair agent’s runtime compacts history once the working set crosses its watermark, a turn delivered after compaction saw a summary digest rather than the raw earlier turns that a single concatenated transcript would place before it. A single concatenated transcript is a faithful training example only when the serving runtime appends every turn without pruning earlier steps, compacting intermediate context, or dropping reasoning scratchpads to save KV-cache memory. Whenever serving prunes or compacts history between steps so that later causal prefixes no longer match a growing transcript, the loader must serialize each invocation as its own training example containing that call’s exact delivered prefix.

↩︎ Watermark-triggered compaction, which replaces earlier turns with a digest between invocations, is established in Context Compaction.

Serializing each call separately carries the same triangular token cost that already priced an append-only trajectory at serving time (equation 2.9), now paid in stored training tokens. If a trajectory spans \(\TurnHorizon\) turns and each turn adds roughly \(\TurnAppendLength\) tokens, the example for turn \(\TurnIndex\) spans about \(\TurnIndex\,\TurnAppendLength\) tokens. Serializing all \(\TurnHorizon\) invocations separately stores \(\TurnAppendLength\,\TurnHorizon(\TurnHorizon+1)/2\) tokens, compared with \(\TurnHorizon\,\TurnAppendLength\) tokens for a single transcript. That is an expansion factor of \((\TurnHorizon+1)/2\), which over a stipulated 30-turn horizon multiplies the stored token volume by 15.5×. An agent’s history-retention policy therefore directly governs training storage and compute. The same fidelity rule applies to the model’s own reasoning text. If serving drops an earlier turn’s rationale before the next call, the reconstructed prefix for that call omits it too. Whether retained rationale is trained as a target or kept as context is a separate masking choice (section 16.4.4).

With serialization_v1 assembled, the pipeline has the tokenized sequences, their conditioning prefixes, and the response spans selected during curation. The next step is deciding which token positions contribute to the training loss, because computing cross-entropy over an entire multi-turn sequence would penalize the model for failing to predict tool outputs and user messages.

16.4 Observation Loss Masking

In serialization_v1, the token array for the repair trajectory still contains the prompt, the malformed proposal e2, the runtime refusal, the returned schema, and the check summary e5 alongside the selected responses e3, e4, and e6. A loader that selects every assistant token trains the model on the malformed proposal e2 alongside its correction e4, undoing curation, while a loader that computes loss across all tokens trains the policy to predict environment replies. Serialization turns the interaction into tokens; the loss mask decides which of those tokens drive gradient updates.

16.4.1 The masked objective

Let \(\SerializedTrainingExample=(\SequenceToken_1,\ldots,\SequenceToken_{\SequenceLength})\) be one serialized example, with an initial context or boundary token at \(\SequenceToken_1\), and let \(\EligibleTargetPositionSet\subseteq\{2,\ldots,\SequenceLength\}\) contain the policy-token positions selected for imitation. Which positions belong to \(\EligibleTargetPositionSet\) depends on the admitted response spans and the training objective rather than token authorship alone. With the binary mask \(\PositionLossMask_{t}=\IndicatorFunction\{\TokenIndex\in\EligibleTargetPositionSet\}\) and \(\PositionLossMask_1=0\), the mean masked cross-entropy for an example with at least one selected target is

\[\MaskedFinetuningLoss(\ModelParameters)=-\frac{1}{\ActiveTargetTokenCount}\sum_{t=2}^{\SequenceLength}\PositionLossMask_{t}\log\Policy(\SequenceToken_t\mid \TokenPrefix),\qquad \ActiveTargetTokenCount=\sum_{t=2}^{\SequenceLength}\PositionLossMask_{t}>0. \tag{1}\]

Each selected token is predicted from the recorded prefix \(\TokenPrefix\), which includes earlier actions, observations, and admitted mistakes, rather than from fresh rollouts sampled during training. This regime is teacher forcing1 (Williams and Zipser 1989), and it lets recovery prefixes that contain earlier failures serve as conditioning context whenever the trajectory satisfies the curation contract.

1 Teacher Forcing: Williams and Zipser (1989) named the technique for recurrent networks, feeding the recorded target back as the next input instead of the network’s own output. Because every prefix is recorded rather than generated, a causal model computes all of an example’s next-token predictions in one parallel forward pass, which is why imitating a trajectory costs one pass per example rather than one sampled rollout per decision.

Williams, Ronald J., and David Zipser. 1989. “A Learning Algorithm for Continually Running Fully Recurrent Neural Networks.” Neural Computation 1 (2): 270–80. https://doi.org/10.1162/neco.1989.1.2.270.

In the running repair trajectory, target_mask_v1 maps the reviewed response spans onto the token array of serialization_v1, separating target positions from conditioning context (table 3). Because changing a tokenizer vocabulary, chat template, or text transformation can shift token boundaries, the pipeline regenerates and checks masks against the final serialized tokens rather than copying offsets from an earlier stage.

When an example has no selected targets (\(\ActiveTargetTokenCount=0\)), the loss in equation 1 is undefined, so the loader must drop the example or route it to a declared fallback rather than counting it as a completed demonstration. How examples of different lengths are weighted against one another inside a batch is a separate choice developed in section 16.5.

Definition 16.1: Observation loss masking

Observation loss masking excludes supplied observations from the direct token-prediction loss while retaining the permitted observation context needed to train selected policy responses.

  1. Significance: Focuses the optimization objective on the policy’s conditional distribution \(\Policy(\TrajectoryAction_{t}\mid \TrajectoryState_{t})\), so verbose environment output cannot dilute the gradient on policy actions regardless of how much context observations occupy (notebook 16.1 quantifies this dilution).
  2. Distinction: Masked tokens stay in the input context and participate in self-attention; only their next-token loss terms are zeroed. Policy authorship alone does not make a token a target, because refused attempts or unsafe actions are also masked even when emitted in the assistant role.
  3. Common pitfall: Selecting an entire multi-turn tool exchange only because the tool call succeeded, which trains the model to predict external environment responses.

A deployed agent trajectory interleaves two different generators: the agent policy and the external environment. Training without observation masking forces one set of weights to fit both distributions at once:

  1. The agent policy \(\Policy(\TrajectoryAction_{t}\mid \TrajectoryState_{t})\), which maps prior observations and instructions to tool calls or answers.
  2. An environment dynamics model \(\EnvironmentTransitionDistribution(\TrajectoryState_{t+1}\mid \TrajectoryState_{t}, \TrajectoryAction_{t})\), which predicts the outputs returned by shells, databases, or remote APIs.

Because real tools run during deployment and return actual outputs, spending model capacity to predict environment returns is wasted work, and supervising the environment distribution causes two concrete failures:

  1. Loss dilution: Under token-averaged cross-entropy, unmasking expands the denominator from \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount\) to all \(\SequenceLength-1\) causal prediction terms (every stored position after the initial boundary). Each decision token’s gradient weight shrinks by the ratio of selected targets to predicted positions (computed in notebook 16.1), while the rest of the loss budget goes to file paths, stack traces, compiler dumps, and JSON payloads. Repetitive boilerplate pulls the average loss down, while novel stack traces or file contents dominate the gradient; in either case, the average stops measuring the policy.
  2. Role confusion: When trained to predict the tokens that follow a tool call, the policy learns that tool outputs continue the same autoregressive stream. At serving time, it can keep generating past its own tool call and write a fabricated tool result instead of emitting the stop token that hands control back to the runtime. Because the runtime rejects unauthenticated observations (section 16.3), those fabricated tokens are discarded, wasting output budget and stalling the turn.

The full repair record selects three responses (e3, e4, and e6). In the simplified view of figure 1, which omits the schema request e3 and its returned schema, only two of the seven segments shown (the corrected tool call and the final response) carry \(\PositionLossMask_{t}=1\) and receive direct imitation loss. The prompt, task statement, refused proposal, rejection message, and tool result carry \(\PositionLossMask_{t}=0\) and stay as conditioning context, where gradients from later selected targets still update the weights that encode them.

Observation masking prevents both loss dilution and role confusion at the objective level, while closed-loop trials verify that the trained model stops at tool boundaries. Realizing that masked objective in code requires aligning every mask bit \(\PositionLossMask_{t}\) with the exact prediction step that generates token \(\SequenceToken_t\).

16.4.2 Causal label alignment

An off-by-one shift between input tokens and target masks silently corrupts every tool boundary in a corpus, masking the first token of each selected response while training the policy to predict the first token of the following tool observation. Many training frameworks shift labels by one position inside their own loss function, so shifting them once in the loader and a second time in the loss misaligns every target mask across the dataset. In a causal autoregressive model,2 the hidden state at input position \(\TokenIndex-1\) produces the logits that predict token \(\SequenceToken_t\), so the loader must pair the logits at \(\TokenIndex-1\) with target label \(\SequenceToken_t\) and target mask \(\PositionLossMask_{t}\) rather than with the mask of input token \(\SequenceToken_{t-1}\) (table 4). Checking the aligned labels and masks on a short synthetic example before launching a run catches this bug immediately.

2 Causal Self-Attention: Causal masking in Transformer decoders restricts each token from attending to subsequent positions (Vaswani et al. 2017). The same triangular structure enforces the label shift: the network predicts \(\SequenceToken_t\) using only the representation formed at \(\TokenIndex-1\).

Vaswani, Ashish, Noam Shazeer, Niki Parmar, Jakob Uszkoreit, Llion Jones, Aidan N. Gomez, Lukasz Kaiser, and Illia Polosukhin. 2017. “Attention Is All You Need.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30: 5998–6008.

Let \(\VocabularyTokenLogit_{t-1,\VocabularyToken}\) denote the logit for vocabulary token \(\VocabularyToken\) produced from \(\TokenPrefix\), using an ordinary softmax and no label smoothing. Differentiating equation 1 gives

\[\frac{\partial\MaskedFinetuningLoss}{\partial \VocabularyTokenLogit_{t-1,\VocabularyToken}}=\frac{\PositionLossMask_{t}}{\ActiveTargetTokenCount}\left(\Policy(\VocabularyToken\mid \TokenPrefix)-\IndicatorFunction\{\VocabularyToken=\SequenceToken_t\}\right).\]

When \(\PositionLossMask_{t}=0\), the prediction at position \(\TokenIndex-1\) contributes zero direct gradient, yet token \(\SequenceToken_t\) still shapes later representations through causal self-attention, so gradients from later active targets update the weights that read it. The model conditions on tool outputs without spending capacity trying to generate them, which carries verified trajectory post-training (principle 13) down to individual token positions.

16.4.3 Selected targets and compute

Observations fill 77.1 percent of the input; the loss sits on 4.9 percent.

Because every masked observation token still passes through self-attention, zeroing its loss term removes almost none of the forward-pass compute. The number of selected positions \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount\) sets the denominator of the loss and the gradient weight on each target token, whereas the full stored sequence length \(\SequenceLength\) sets the attention and projection FLOPs. Comparing \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount\) against the \(\SequenceLength-1\) causal prediction terms gives the dilution factor an unmasked loss would suffer, while \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount/\SequenceLength\) gives the fraction of stored positions that carry a target. Notebook 16.1 computes both quantities under two target policies on the same serialized trajectory.

Napkin Math 16.1: Stored tokens and selected targets
Problem: A hypothetical serialized example has the segment counts in table 5. Its curation record excludes some policy-response positions from imitation while retaining them as context. If the optional rationale is either selected or kept as context only, how many positions receive direct loss, what fraction of the stored input do they occupy, and by how much would an unmasked loss dilute each of them?

Variables: Exactly 160 of the other-policy-response positions belong to the refused proposal and stay context; every other response position is selected. The rationale is reviewed for either target policy, both policies see the same input, the initial position belongs to the prompt, and there is no padding.

Math: The disjoint segments sum to 16,384 stored tokens, of which observations occupy 77.1 percent. Removing the refused positions leaves 960 \(-\) 160 \(=\) 800 selected response targets, and selecting the rationale adds 1,400 more. The first stored boundary has no preceding-position prediction, so an unmasked causal average divides by 16,383 prediction terms instead of \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount\). Each selected target’s coefficient therefore shrinks by 16,383/800, a factor of 20.5× (7.4× when the rationale is selected). Under each target policy, table 6 sets \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount\) against the stored input it is drawn from.

Result: Both masks place the whole loss on a small minority of stored positions and leave the context identical; they differ only in whether the rationale is a target.

Systems insight: Because the dilution factor scales inversely with the selected fraction of predicted positions, it expands rapidly as environment interactions accumulate. Doubling the observation volume while holding response lengths constant escalates dilution from 20.5× to 36.3×. Consequently, observation masking becomes increasingly essential as agents tackle deep, tool-intensive reasoning traces. Masking, however, yields negligible reductions in FLOPs: every masked token must still pass through causal attention layers to form context for subsequent predictions. Only the final language model head projection and cross-entropy loss computation can be bypassed at masked positions.

Recorded repair trajectories show that the hypothetical inventory’s proportions are realistic. Figure 2 splits the transcripts of five published agent configurations on the same repository-repair tasks (Jimenez et al. 2024) by the role that produced each message.

Across the 5 configurations and 2,100 runs, observations make up 56 to 72 percent of the transcript, and model output only 12 to 31 percent. These shares count each message once. Serializing one example per invocation repeats the growing prefix in every example, so the observation share of the stored training input is larger still, approaching the inventory’s proportion. The mask is therefore not a refinement applied to a few verbose tool calls; in recorded repair work it decides the fate of most of the sequence.

16.4.4 Optional rationale as target or context

Even within the model-generated share of a trajectory, masking out a chain-of-thought scratchpad during training while expecting the deployed policy to act without any scratchpad at serving time trains the model on conditioning context it will not have in production. When a dataset includes intermediate reasoning traces or tool-use plans alongside tool calls, the loader must therefore choose among three masking regimes (table 7) that match how the model will be served. Supervising the rationale trains the model to generate its own reasoning before invoking tools. Retaining the rationale under a loss mask treats the reasoning as supplied conditioning context, training the model to act on plans provided by an external orchestrator or user. Stripping the rationale entirely changes the conditional distribution so the policy acts from context alone.

Formally, given context prefix \(\TurnContext_{t}\), rationale tokens \(\RationaleTokens\), and action tokens \(\ResponseActionTokens=(\ResponseActionTokens_1,\ldots,\ResponseActionTokens_n)\), response-only supervision fits the conditional distribution \(\Policy(\ResponseActionTokens_j\mid \TurnContext_{t}, \RationaleTokens, \ActionTokenPrefix)\). If the production serving environment does not supply \(\RationaleTokens\), the deployed model instead evaluates \(\Policy(\ResponseActionTokens_j\mid \TurnContext_{t}, \ActionTokenPrefix)\). Because those are distinct conditional distributions, omitting rationale at serving time is a distillation step that must be evaluated directly against the unconditioned target.

At serving time, either an external planner or the policy’s own autoregressive decoder supplies the reasoning text. When the model generates its own rationale, that text can drift from the training demonstrations, so closed-loop evaluation must measure whether the generated rationale helps enough to earn its output tokens and latency.

16.4.5 Termination and truncated responses

Whether a response carries rationale or only a tool call, its mask must also handle how the turn ends. To teach an agent when to yield control, the training loss must include the authentic policy-generated stop token (such as <|eom_id|> or <|im_end|>) at the end of each complete selected response, rather than confusing it with host delimiters that mark incoming tool payloads. Without direct supervision on the stop token, the policy can keep generating past the end of a valid tool call until it hits the output limit, returning TRUNCATED instead of COMPLETED so the runtime’s quarantining rule withholds the proposal.

↩︎ The normalized TRUNCATED status and the quarantining rule that withholds an incomplete candidate are established in The normalized status envelope.

Appending a synthetic end-of-turn token to a response cut short by a context window or timeout is the training-side twin of the hazard the quarantining rule blocks at serving time, where a truncated rm -rf argument still parses as a complete and broader command. The training-side version is worse, because a policy that has learned to emit the terminator after a partial command produces a COMPLETED status, which the quarantining rule admits. When a turn was truncated in the recorded trace, the loader has two sound options:

  1. Conservative exclusion: Mask the truncated turn entirely (\(\PositionLossMask_{t}=0\)) while keeping earlier complete turns in the trajectory.
  2. Prefix-only supervision: Supervise the valid initial tokens of the truncated turn up to the truncation point, and omit any terminating token.

Whenever trajectories undergo truncation, sliding-window packing, or template changes, the data pipeline recomputes target masks and checks that \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount > 0\). Automated assertions verify the first token of each agent response, the closing turn terminator, the transition into tool observations, refused recovery attempts, and truncated turns before the serialized dataset enters the training loop.

Checkpoint 16.1: Defend the target mask

With per-example loss masking in place, the loader separates conditioning context from imitation targets inside each sequence. Training at scale, however, combines trajectories of widely varying lengths into batches across device buffers, and the batching pipeline must preserve that per-example contract without leaking context across examples or silently skewing their weights.

16.5 Batching Variable-Length Trajectories

Consider a batch where a 30-turn debugging and code-repair trace shares a training buffer with a brief, one-turn file lookup. Under an ordinary lower-triangular causal mask, the lookup can attend directly to the repair trace’s intermediate file contents and tool outputs, context that the lookup policy will never have at serving time. Under token averaging, the multi-turn repair trace also outweighs the brief lookup by the ratio of their active target tokens.

Constructing a batch of variable-length trajectories therefore pairs two systems choices: how much gradient weight each trajectory receives in the batch loss, and how multiple sequences share physical buffer memory without leaking context across sequence boundaries. Sequence packing is only a memory layout; a faithful implementation keeps both the declared loss weighting and each example’s isolated predictive context.

16.5.1 Loss normalization across variable-length examples

Let a batch contain \(\BatchSize\) examples, where example \(\BatchExampleIndex\) has stored length \(\SequenceLength^{(\BatchExampleIndex)}\) (including context) and \({\ActiveTargetTokenCount}_{\BatchExampleIndex}>0\) selected target tokens under the mask of section 16.4, with mean masked negative log-likelihood \(\LossFunction_{\BatchExampleIndex}\). The two standard batch objectives weight the long repair trace and the one-turn file lookup differently:

\[\TokenAveragedBatchLoss= \sum_{\BatchExampleIndex=1}^{\BatchSize}\frac{{\ActiveTargetTokenCount}_{\BatchExampleIndex}}{\sum_{k=1}^{\BatchSize}{\ActiveTargetTokenCount}_k}\LossFunction_{\BatchExampleIndex}, \tag{2}\]

\[\ExampleAveragedBatchLoss=\frac{1}{\BatchSize}\sum_{\BatchExampleIndex=1}^{\BatchSize}\LossFunction_{\BatchExampleIndex}. \tag{3}\]

Token averaging in equation 2 gives equal weight to every selected token loss, whereas example averaging in equation 3 gives equal weight to every example mean. Both count only selected targets, never context or padding, and any record with no selected targets is excluded from both objectives. Because a serialized example may be a single invocation rather than a full task, example averaging equalizes serialized records rather than underlying tasks, while token averaging weights each record in proportion to its active target count.

The difference becomes critical when per-invocation serialization splits one multi-turn interaction into separate training records. If one of \(\BatchSize\) initial examples is split into \(\TurnHorizon\) per-invocation records while every other example stays unsplit, example averaging raises that trajectory’s share of the batch loss from \(1/\BatchSize\) to \(\TurnHorizon/(\BatchSize-1+\TurnHorizon)\), giving it \(\TurnHorizon\) times the weight of each unsplit example. Under token averaging, the trajectory’s aggregate share stays unchanged as long as its selected targets are partitioned disjointly across the \(\TurnHorizon\) records. Even though the record for a later turn repeats earlier assistant responses inside its conditioning prefix, the loader masks those earlier responses (\(\PositionLossMask_{t}=0\)) and sets \(\PositionLossMask_{t}=1\) only on that invocation’s final response, so repeating prefix context does not repeat active targets. A corpus mixture specified in selected target tokens, the unit in which Records, stored tokens, and selected targets fixes class shares, therefore reaches the parameter update unchanged only under token averaging. Example averaging, or a per-invocation split that leaves earlier assistant turns unmasked and selects the same target twice, changes the realized mixture (table 8).

Preserving the coefficients in table 8 across a cluster requires matching distributed reduction. A large batch is split into microbatches (slices processed sequentially before a parameter step) and spread across data-parallel workers (each holding a model copy and processing its shard of the batch). Computing a true global token average therefore requires summing active token losses and active token counts across all microbatches and workers before dividing. Averaging local microbatch means directly matches the global token average only when every microbatch has the exact same active token count. When variable-length trajectories make those counts differ, workers must reduce the raw numerators and denominators before taking their ratio.

16.5.2 Example isolation in packed buffers

Accurate loss reduction settles how the batch weights the file lookup and the 30-turn repair trace, but not how they share device memory. Packing both trajectories into one contiguous buffer under an ordinary causal mask lets every query in the lookup read the repair trace’s file contents and tool observations. That leak across packed sequences is cross-contamination,3 and preventing it is the primary correctness obligation of sequence packing (Krell et al. 2021).

3 Cross-Contamination: Krell et al. (2021) used the term for attention and label leakage between sequences packed into one buffer, and showed that masks, position resets, and per-sequence loss handling make a packed model mathematically equivalent to the unpacked one. The equivalence is the property to test: a packed loader that trains faster but lets examples read one another has changed the learning problem, and no loss curve will show it.

Krell, Mario Michael, Matej Kosec, Sergio P. Perez, and Andrew Fitzgibbon. 2021. “Efficient Sequence Packing Without Cross-Contamination: Accelerating Large Language Models Without Impacting Performance.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2107.02027, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2107.02027.
Definition 16.2: Sequence packing

Sequence packing places several variable-length training examples in shared buffers while preserving their intended information boundaries and loss targets.

  1. Significance: Uniform padding allocates the longest example’s length to every row, reducing batch occupancy to the ratio of mean to maximum sequence length. Sequence packing reclaims this wasted capacity (notebook 16.2).
  2. Distinction: Independent trajectories share physical memory buffers without sharing predictive context; multi-turn interactions within an individual trajectory retain access to their complete history.
  3. Common pitfall: Applying a standard causal mask across concatenated sequences, allowing later examples to attend illegitimately to earlier ones.

Let \(\QueryBufferPosition\) and \(\KeyBufferPosition\) denote buffer positions, \(\QueryVector_i\) and \(\KeyVector_j\) their query and key vectors, and \(\AttentionHeadDimension\) the attention-head dimension. The pre-softmax attention scores \(\text{score}_{i,j}\) enforce both causality and example membership:

\[\text{score}_{i,j}=\begin{cases} \QueryVector_i\KeyVector_j^T/\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension} & \text{if }\operatorname{example}(\QueryBufferPosition)=\operatorname{example}(\KeyBufferPosition)\text{ and }\KeyBufferPosition\le \QueryBufferPosition,\\ -\infty & \text{otherwise}. \end{cases} \tag{4}\]

Under an ordinary causal mask, every query in a later example can score keys in an earlier one. The block-diagonal mask sets each of those cross-example entries to \(-\infty\) before the softmax, and excludes trailing padding keys the same way (figure 3). Variable-length attention kernels (Dao 2023) enforce this without materializing a dense \(-\infty\) matrix. The loader hands the kernel the example boundaries as cumulative offsets, \(0\), \(\PackedExampleLength_1\), \(\PackedExampleLength_1+\PackedExampleLength_2\), and so on through the buffer’s total length (\(\BatchSize+1\) entries for \(\BatchSize\) examples), so that example \(\PackedExampleIndex\) occupies one contiguous interval of positions. The kernel then computes scores only for query and key pairs that fall inside the same interval. Whether a given kernel also skips the computation for cross-example blocks of the score matrix, and how much time that saves, is an implementation property to measure on the target accelerator. The mask is the correctness requirement; skipped work is a throughput bonus that must be shown, not assumed.

Dao, Tri. 2023. “FlashAttention-2: Faster Attention with Better Parallelism and Work Partitioning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.08691.

One long trajectory holds padded occupancy to 19.1 percent; packing reaches 76.6 percent.

Loss computation needs the same boundary isolation: shifting next-token labels across a concatenated buffer without resetting at example boundaries trains the final token of one example to predict the initial prompt token of the next. Each packed example’s initial prompt context must stay unselected (\(\PositionLossMask = 0\)), and every shifted prediction-target pair must stay inside its own example. The block-diagonal attention mask isolates visible context, while the per-example target mask isolates the loss gradient.

Napkin Math 16.2: Stored occupancy and target weighting
Problem: A batch holds one long trajectory among short ones. How much allocated sequence space does packing save over uniform padding, and how differently do the two loss normalizations weight a long and a short example?

Variables: Eight hypothetical examples have stored lengths \([32{,}768,\ 8{,}192,\ 4{,}096,\ 2{,}048,\ 1{,}024,\ 1{,}024,\ 512,\ 512]\). Each packed buffer holds \(32{,}768\) positions. Separately, two examples have \(256\) and \(8{,}192\) selected targets.

Math: Padding eight rows to the maximum allocates \(8\times32{,}768\) positions, or 262,144, for 50,176 stored tokens. Packing places the longest example alone in one buffer and the remaining 17,408 tokens in a second buffer. The two buffers allocate \(2\times32{,}768\) positions, or 65,536. Dividing stored tokens by allocated positions gives an occupancy of 19.1 percent with padding (the mean length of 6,272 divided by the maximum of 32,768) and 76.6 percent with packing. Packing allocates 4× fewer positions.

For the separate target-count illustration, token averaging gives coefficients \(256/(256+8{,}192)\) and \(8{,}192/(256+8{,}192)\), or 3.03 percent and 96.97 percent on the example means. Their ratio, \(8{,}192/256\), is 32×. Example averaging instead assigns each mean \(1/2\).

Systems insight: Padded occupancy is the ratio of mean to maximum length, so the single longest trajectory sets the waste: one 32,768-token example holds this batch to 19.1 percent, and packing raises it to 76.6 percent. Occupancy counts context and targets together, so the throughput it buys is measured on the actual attention kernel. Normalization is a separate knob with an equally large effect. Token averaging gives the long example 32× the short one’s weight, and example averaging gives them equal weight. Choose token averaging when every selected token should count equally, and example averaging when a few long trajectories should not dominate the update.

16.5.3 Position conventions and equivalence checks

Attention and label masks settle what each packed position reads and predicts, but each token also carries a position index. If the loader leaves buffer-wide position indices unreset, the second packed example starts at offset \(\PackedExampleLength_1\) instead of zero, which shifts its logits under any position scheme that is not shift-invariant. Resetting positions to zero at each example boundary avoids the shift directly: for zero-based buffer offsets \(\PackedExampleOffset_k\) and \(\PackedExampleOffset_{k+1}\) bounding example \(\PackedExampleIndex\),

\[\operatorname{pos}[\TokenIndex]=\TokenIndex-\PackedExampleOffset_k\quad\text{for }\TokenIndex\in[\PackedExampleOffset_k,\PackedExampleOffset_{k+1}).\]

Rotary position embeddings (RoPE), a widely used scheme that encodes position by rotating query and key vectors through position-dependent angles, are the case where the offset provably makes no difference, so for them resetting is an operational convention rather than a mathematical requirement. In the fixed-frequency formulation, position \(\QueryBufferPosition\) applies a rotation \(\RotaryRotationMatrix_i\) to a query or key vector. Shifting both positions by a common offset \(\CommonPositionShift\) leaves their relative rotation unchanged:

\[\RotaryRotationMatrix_{i+\CommonPositionShift}^T\RotaryRotationMatrix_{j+\CommonPositionShift}=\RotaryRotationMatrix_{j-i}=\RotaryRotationMatrix_i^T\RotaryRotationMatrix_j.\]

A common offset therefore leaves within-example attention scores unchanged, a direct consequence of the relative-position construction of RoPE (Su et al. 2024). Absolute-position features, position-dependent frequency scaling, and floating-point non-associativity can still compromise equivalence for a particular architecture, so no position scheme is exempt from checking.

Su, Jianlin, Murtadha Ahmed, Yu Lu, Shengfeng Pan, Wen Bo, and Yunfeng Liu. 2024. “RoFormer: Enhanced Transformer with Rotary Position Embedding.” Neurocomputing 568: 127063. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neucom.2023.127063.

The general answer is therefore a test rather than a proof. The correctness test compares selected-token logits and the evaluated loss for the same examples processed separately and packed, with stochastic layers disabled and numerical tolerances explicitly stated. Perturbing one example must leave every other example’s logits unchanged. This test exercises attention boundaries, label shifting, position IDs, and the chosen denominator together. When it passes, the loader trains the demonstrated problem cleanly. The trained policy, however, still has to act in contexts its own choices produce.

16.6 Exposure Bias

Suppose a repair policy trained on this corpus is placed in a fresh bounded trial and meets a version conflict instead of the malformed-argument rejection recorded in trace_r19_01_redacted_v1. A plausible failure is that the policy repeats its previously accepted edit without first querying compatible state evidence. The runtime’s conditional-update guard then blocks the stale write, and the trial exhausts its execution allowance without completing the repair. The training corpus never included that state, so nothing in it taught a correction. While serialization and loss masking preserve the demonstrated decision problem, production deployment dictates which states the agent actually encounters. A policy trained only under teacher forcing never observes its own compounding errors during optimization. In standard text generation, an unexpected token merely drifts stylistic phrasing. In an autonomous agent, an errant action mutates the external environment and pushes the agent into states that the demonstration corpus never covered.

Definition 16.3: Exposure bias

Exposure bias is the mismatch between training a sequential policy on reference prefixes and running it on prefixes produced by its own earlier outputs (Bengio et al. 2015; Ranzato et al. 2016).

  1. Significance: It converts a per-step error rate into a horizon-dependent cost. Unchecked horizon compounding (Reliability and Cost over a Horizon) already makes the failure probability roughly \(\TurnHorizon\PerStepErrorRate\) for a small per-step rate \(\PerStepErrorRate\). When that rate is measured solely on reference states, the best available bound on excess task cost is \(\TurnHorizon^2\PerStepErrorRate\) (theorem 16.1), one further factor of \(\TurnHorizon\).
  2. Distinction: It reflects a structural property of the training distribution rather than an isolated mistake. A policy can err on states the corpus covers and recover on states it never showed.
  3. Common pitfall: Interpreting low offline validation loss on recorded prefixes as evidence that deployed execution will succeed. Offline loss samples only the reference trajectory, revealing nothing about the states the deployed policy visits once it deviates.

4 Exposure Bias: Ranzato et al. (2016) introduced the name for text generators trained on reference words and run on their own. Control researchers had met the same effect earlier as the compounding error of behavioral cloning, which Ross and Bagnell (2010) bounded. The two literatures describe one mechanism, and agents inherit the control version: a wrong action changes the environment, not just the phrasing.

Ranzato, Marc’Aurelio, Sumit Chopra, Michael Auli, and Wojciech Zaremba. 2016. “Sequence Level Training with Recurrent Neural Networks.” 4th International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Ross, Stéphane, and J. Andrew Bagnell. 2010. “Efficient Reductions for Imitation Learning.” Proceedings of the Thirteenth International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS) 9: 661–68.

Offline training corpora can include recovery trajectories, allowing an agent that departs from an ideal demonstration to re-enter a familiar state or discover an alternate valid solution. Exposure bias,4 however, concerns how thoroughly the corpus covers the state distribution generated by the deployed policy itself, including unexpected edge states like the version conflict.

16.6.1 State distributions and imitation regret

An idealized finite-horizon model makes the cost of missing states like the version conflict exact. This derivation follows the imitation-learning notation locally. Let \(\TurnIndex\) count discrete decision steps rather than token positions. Let \(\DecisionProcessState\) denote the complete environment state of the decision process (including interaction history and system variables the agent cannot directly observe, whereas \(\TrajectoryState_{t}\) in definition 16.1 named only the policy’s visible token context). View the trained policy \(\pi\) (corresponding to \(\Policy\)) and the deterministic expert reference \(\pi^*\) as maps from states to actions over a \(\TurnHorizon\)-step horizon under identical initial states and transition dynamics. If \(\StateDistribution^t\) is the distribution of states visited before step \(\TurnIndex\) under \(\pi\), the horizon-averaged state distribution is \(\StateDistribution = \TurnHorizon^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^\TurnHorizon \StateDistribution^t\).

Let \(\ImmediateTaskCostScore(\DecisionProcessState,\PolicyAction)\in[0,1]\) be a normalized immediate task cost and \(\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi)\) its expected sum over the horizon. Let \(\LossFunction(\DecisionProcessState,\pi)=\Pr_{\PolicyAction\sim\pi(\cdot\mid \DecisionProcessState)}[\PolicyAction\ne\pi^*(\DecisionProcessState)]\) measure the probability of choosing an action different from the reference at decision time, a conservative proxy for task error since several distinct actions may be acceptable, and a decision-step quantity distinct from token-level cross-entropy. Finally, the cost-to-go \(\ReferenceCostToGo(\DecisionProcessState,\PolicyAction)\) is the expected cost from step \(\TurnIndex\) through \(\TurnHorizon\) after taking \(\PolicyAction\) in \(\DecisionProcessState\) and following the reference \(\pi^*\) thereafter.

Theorem 16.1: Imitation regret bounds
The horizon penalty of imitation depends on which states the error is measured on. For a policy \(\pi\) imitating a deterministic reference \(\pi^*\) over \(\TurnHorizon\) steps with per-step cost in \([0,1]\), Ross et al. (2011) bound the excess task cost in two ways.

Reference-state error (their Theorem 2.1, first proved by Ross and Bagnell (2010)). With disagreement measured on the reference policy’s states, \(\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate=\mathbb E_{\DecisionProcessState\sim \ReferenceStateDistribution}[\LossFunction(\DecisionProcessState,\pi)]\),

\[\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi)-\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi^*)\le \min\{\TurnHorizon,\;\TurnHorizon^2\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate\}. \tag{5}\]

Learner-state error (their Theorem 2.2). With disagreement measured on the learner’s states, \(\LearnerStateDisagreementRate=\mathbb E_{\DecisionProcessState\sim \StateDistribution}[\LossFunction(\DecisionProcessState,\pi)]\), and every deviation followed by reference recovery costing at most \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) extra, \(\ReferenceCostToGo(\DecisionProcessState,\PolicyAction)-\ReferenceCostToGo(\DecisionProcessState,\pi^*(\DecisionProcessState))\le \DeviationDisadvantage\) at every learner-reachable state,

\[\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi)-\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi^*)\le \min\{\TurnHorizon,\;\DeviationDisadvantage \TurnHorizon\LearnerStateDisagreementRate\}. \tag{6}\]

  • Horizon (\(\TurnHorizon\)): decision steps in the task; because each step costs at most one, \(\TurnHorizon\) also caps both bounds.
  • State distributions (\(\ReferenceStateDistribution\), \(\StateDistribution\)): the environment states the reference or the learner visits, averaged over the horizon.
  • Disagreement rates (\(\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate\), \(\LearnerStateDisagreementRate\)): probability of an action other than the reference’s, averaged over reference-visited vs. learner-visited states.
  • Deviation disadvantage (\(\DeviationDisadvantage\ge0\)): the largest extra expected cost of one deviation followed by reference recovery, an assumption about the task rather than a measured retry count.

The same error rate therefore carries different horizon penalties. At \(\TurnHorizon=\) 30 and a 2 percent disagreement rate, the reference-state bound is 18 cost units and the learner-state bound with \(\DeviationDisadvantage=\) 1 is 0.6, a gap of 30×, which is \(\TurnHorizon/\DeviationDisadvantage\). The linear bound holds only while \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) stays independent of \(\TurnHorizon\); one action that can spoil the rest of the task makes \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) grow with \(\TurnHorizon\) and restores the quadratic dependence.

Both bounds have a direct physical reading. For the reference-state bound, couple the environment randomness of the two policies until they first choose different actions. Across \(\TurnHorizon\) decision steps, the probability that the learner ever diverges from the reference path is at most the sum of per-step disagreement probabilities, \(\TurnHorizon\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate\). Because an offline corpus contains only reference-visited states, this coupling argument makes no assumptions about how the learner behaves once it leaves the reference trajectory, so a single divergence can cost up to one unit on each of the remaining \(\TurnHorizon\) steps and multiply \(\TurnHorizon\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate\) by a second factor of \(\TurnHorizon\). When the corpus instead covers the learner’s own state distribution with disagreement rate \(\LearnerStateDisagreementRate\), a slip at step \(\TurnIndex\) moves the agent into a state where it still knows how to follow the reference recovery. Each deviation therefore adds at most \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) extra cost rather than spoiling the remaining horizon, and summing that penalty across \(\TurnHorizon\) steps yields \(\DeviationDisadvantage\TurnHorizon\LearnerStateDisagreementRate\). Conditional error bounds in trajectory imitation derives the reference-state bound by coupling and the learner-state bound through telescoping policy comparisons.

Unchecked runtime step errors compound as \((1-\PerStepErrorRate)^\TurnHorizon\) (equation 1.1), and for small \(\PerStepErrorRate\) this is close to \(1-\TurnHorizon\PerStepErrorRate\), so the chance of some failure grows roughly as \(\TurnHorizon\PerStepErrorRate\). Theorem 16.1 adds the training-side consequence, since imitation whose error is measured on the reference distribution is bounded only by \(\TurnHorizon^2\PerStepErrorRate\), a second factor of \(\TurnHorizon\). The two rates are analogous rather than identical. The introduction’s \(\PerStepErrorRate\) is a probability of error, while the disagreement rate counts every departure from the reference, harmless ones included, and so upper-bounds that error probability.

Same 2 percent error: 18 cost units on reference states, 0.6 on learner states.

The bounds are stated in normalized cost units. If each step spent off the reference path costs one unit and every other step costs nothing, the reference-state bound of 18 units caps the expected number of wasted turns at 18 of 30. The learner-state bound of 0.6 caps it below one. Wasted turns reach the familiar evaluation quantities directly, since they raise the expected attempt cost in \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) and, once they exhaust the turn budget, lower the acceptance probability. The deviation disadvantage \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) is an assumption about the task and its reference, not a property of the trained learner; it asks how much extra cost one slip adds when the reference takes over afterward. A repair edit that the runtime refuses before dispatch, or that a version guard blocks, costs a small, fixed number of extra turns to correct however long the task is (in the recorded trace, one schema inspection and one reissued call). A small \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) that does not grow with \(\TurnHorizon\) is therefore plausible for the repair agent. Retrospective recovery records help only to the extent that they populate the training corpus with learner-visited states and keep \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) small, but an offline dataset alone cannot guarantee that either assumption holds during deployment.

Napkin Math 16.3: The same error rate under different assumptions
Problem: An illustrative task has horizon 30 and immediate cost bounded between zero and one. Compare a 2 percent disagreement rate on reference-policy states with the same numerical rate on learner-policy states. What changes if the latter also satisfies a deviation-disadvantage bound of \(\DeviationDisadvantage=\) 1, and how accurate would offline imitation have to be to match it?

Math: The reference-state bound multiplies \(\TurnHorizon^2=\) 900 by \(\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate=\) 0.02, giving 18 normalized cost units, below the cap of 30. The learner-state bound multiplies \(\DeviationDisadvantage \TurnHorizon=\) 30 by the same rate, giving 0.6. If the disadvantage instead reaches \(\DeviationDisadvantage=\TurnHorizon\), the learner bound returns to 18. Matching the learner-state bound on reference states requires \(\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate\) equal to that bound divided by \(\TurnHorizon^2\), about 0.07 percent.

Result: At this horizon the two bounds differ by 30× for the same measured error rate.

Systems insight: The gap is \(\TurnHorizon/\DeviationDisadvantage\), so it grows linearly with the horizon. Offline imitation must be \(\TurnHorizon/\DeviationDisadvantage\) times more accurate on reference states to carry the guarantee that labeling learner-visited states provides. The reference-state bound also saturates at its cap once \(\TurnHorizon\ge 1/\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate\), here 50 steps, beyond which it constrains nothing. Long-horizon agents with cheap, recoverable deviations are therefore where on-policy labeling pays most. When one deviation can spoil the task (\(\DeviationDisadvantage\) near \(\TurnHorizon\)), the learner-state bound is no better than the quadratic one, and the runtime checks that invariant closure (principle 1) requires carry the reliability.

16.6.2 Dataset aggregation

A fixed offline corpus never shows the policy how to recover from states that only the learner’s own actions reach, such as the version conflict. Dataset Aggregation (DAgger) closes this coverage gap by rolling out trajectories under successive learned policies. It queries an authorized reference labeler at the states they actually visit, adds those labeled transitions to the training corpus, and retrains the policy5 (Ross et al. 2011). The resulting dataset is on-policy data, drawn from the state distribution of the policy being trained. Executing a learner action in the environment and generating its reference label are separate operations, and early collection rounds mix them. In round \(\LabelingRoundIndex\), the collection worker executes the reference action with probability \(\ReferenceMixingProbability_k\) and the learner’s action otherwise, decaying \(\ReferenceMixingProbability_k\) toward zero across rounds. This schedule shifts the collected states toward the learner’s own distribution, where theorem 16.1 measures \(\LearnerStateDisagreementRate\). Because aggregation broadens coverage without making the training and learner distributions identical, and because the linear bound holds only where \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) stays small, each retrained round is checked on fresh rollouts rather than assumed to satisfy the bound.

5 DAgger’s Mixing Schedule: Ross et al. (2011) require only that the average reference-execution probability tend to zero over rounds, and report that the simplest schedule, executing the reference only in the first round, often performs best. The systems cost sits elsewhere: every visited state needs a reference label on demand, so the labeler is an online service in the collection loop rather than a one-time annotation pass.

Ross, Stéphane, Geoffrey Gordon, and J. Andrew Bagnell. 2011. “A Reduction of Imitation Learning and Structured Prediction to No-Regret Online Learning.” Proceedings of the Fourteenth International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS) 15: 627–35.

When the learner reaches an off-distribution state like the version conflict, the labeler (typically a larger foundation model or a search procedure with an expanded budget) proposes a corrective action, which can itself be wrong. To avoid training recovery in the wrong direction, the curation pipeline executes the proposed step inside an isolated sandbox and admits the label only if automated verification passes under the predicates of Admission predicates. A passing continuation confirms that the label works, not that it is the only valid action. Because steps that look superficially valid can still rely on ungrounded assumptions or violated preconditions, the pipeline records ground-truth observations, enforces tool contracts, and tracks lineage for disputed labels. After retraining shifts the policy’s visited states, the updated checkpoint is evaluated on fresh bounded rollouts and held-out suites rather than on the accumulated training buffer.

Waiting for live rollouts to reach every rare failure mode is slow when the harness team already knows which infrastructure faults will occur. Fault injection supplies those off-path states deliberately rather than waiting for the learner to reach them. The fault conditions a collection fixture must reproduce faithfully, and the causal distinction each one preserves, are established in Fault injection. The training-specific question is where the mask falls. In a fault-injected record, the synthesized error is presented as an observation with loss mask \(\PositionLossMask_{t} = 0\), and the diagnosis and corrective tool invocation that follow are targets with \(\PositionLossMask_{t} = 1\), so the gradient lands on decision logic that nominal demonstrations never exercise. The selected targets inherit the fixture’s causal distinctions, so a record that answers a lost acknowledgment with a blind retry teaches the duplicate effect that settlement before retry (principle 8) exists to prevent. Fault injection covers the failures the harness designers anticipated, whereas on-policy collection uncovers the ones the model’s own behavior produces (figure 4). Both are choices of behavior source in the sense of section 16.2.2, and both still need a step-level label for every selected decision. Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards drops that requirement by scoring fresh rollouts against terminal outcomes instead.

↪ Optimizing a policy against verifiable outcomes on fresh rollouts, without step-level labels, is developed in Foundations of Verifiable Rewards.

Checkpoint 16.2: Coverage after faithful imitation

State coverage encompasses both environment variables and the tool contracts presented at each decision step. An agent may recover reliably from familiar runtime exceptions yet fail persistently by emitting deprecated argument names following an interface update. Schema robustness tests this narrower sensitivity to structured tool definitions.

16.7 Schema Robustness

Suppose a deployment updates its read_file tool so the active interface requires a string argument named target_uri, forbids extraneous keys, and resolves paths relative to an authorized fixture directory. Even when training trajectories such as trace_r19_01_redacted_v1 taught the repair agent to inspect a tool schema after a refusal, a corpus that showed only one static read_file signature across thousands of examples lets the policy memorize legacy parameter keys in its weights instead of reading the contract supplied in the prompt context:

{
  "proposed_call": {
    "name": "read_file",
    "arguments": {"file_path": "src/pool.py"}
  },
  "current_argument_schema": {
    "type": "object",
    "properties": {"target_uri": {"type": "string"}},
    "required": ["target_uri"],
    "additionalProperties": false
  }
}

This proposal violates the presented schema because it omits the required target_uri property and supplies the disallowed file_path key. Because the agent runtime validates invocations before dispatch, the schema validator rejects the call immediately; the compatible payload is {"target_uri": "src/pool.py"}, and whether that path exists and whether the agent holds authorization to access it remain independent downstream runtime checks. Consulting a schema in a recorded trace and adhering to an altered interface contract at serving time are therefore two distinct capabilities, and testing whether a model genuinely conditions on the presented schema requires perturbing the interface definition while holding the underlying task fixed.

Emitting stale parameter keys is a clear failure mode of interface memorization, but attributing it to trajectory fine-tuning requires controlled comparison, because base foundation models also make schema errors when prompted zero-shot. Isolating the effect of fine-tuning demands evaluating both the base and fine-tuned policies under identical prompts, sampling parameters, and tool schemas; section 16.11 formalizes the metrics and comparison protocol that establish this attribution.

16.7.1 Schema augmentation

To keep the policy from memorizing file_path, schema augmentation constructs training trajectories with declared interface mutations so the model must read the argument names from the prompt context rather than from its weights. That transformation requires more than a string find-and-replace on tool-call arguments: renaming file_path to target_uri in the call while leaving file_path in the prompt schema or in an earlier error message trains the policy on a contradictory transcript. Every part of the decision context (schema definitions, selected calls, mock observations, and error messages) must change together, and the resulting derived trajectories are tracked under the lineage rules of Derived records. Conditioning policies directly on delivered interface contracts is well established: incorporating retrieved API documentation into training prompts lets fine-tuned models adapt when documentation changes after deployment (Patil et al. 2023). Table 9 pairs each schema transformation with the consistency check that keeps the resulting trajectory coherent.

Patil, Shishir G., Tianjun Zhang, Xin Wang, and Joseph E. Gonzalez. 2023. “Gorilla: Large Language Model Connected with Massive APIs.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2305.15334, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2305.15334.

↩︎ The rule that a transformed trajectory becomes a new derived version bound to its parent trace is established in Derived records.

In the read-tool example, a meaning-preserving rename needs an adapter that maps target_uri to the underlying file operation. Every prior tool output, system prompt, or turn that mentions file_path must be updated across the context, so the derived record shows a coherent interaction without claiming those bytes appeared in the original historical run. Whenever a transformation changes return values, ordering, defaults, or side effects, the pipeline generates fresh execution evidence, either by replaying the trajectory in an instrumented sandbox or by synthesizing verified responses, rather than splicing stale historical observations onto modified calls.

These transformations carry direct storage and compute costs: renaming identifiers shifts subword boundaries and token counts, adding distractor tools lengthens the prompt at every turn (raising training FLOPs and inference KV-cache footprints), and every synthetic variant expands the corpus that verification pipelines must check. Teams therefore version each transformation pipeline, track token expansion ratios, and test whether the schema transfer gains pay for the added training and review cost.

16.7.2 Transfer evaluation

Measuring whether schema augmentation actually prevented memorization requires separating three evaluation regimes: new tasks on familiar tool schemas, familiar tasks on renamed or restructured interfaces, and new tasks on unfamiliar tool families. Mixing these regimes in one score hides where a policy breaks. Evaluation splits must hold out task answers and their synthetic variants, whereas reusing familiar tool schemas across train and test splits is not contamination when the benchmark is testing task transfer under a known interface.

↩︎ The isolation of held-out task answers, and the rule that transformed records inherit their parent’s split group, are established in Held-out evidence protection.

Within each regime, the evaluation harness separates structural schema conformance, tool selection, and task completion, because a call can pass the JSON Schema validator while invoking the wrong tool, targeting an unauthorized path, or supplying arguments that do not fix the bug.
