Comparing augmented and unaugmented checkpoints under an equal token budget across both familiar and renamed schemas shows whether the model gained interface robustness without losing task performance, while the runtime continues to validate every call before execution. Supervised imitation and schema augmentation together teach an agent to emit valid calls that match demonstrated behavior; when several valid calls all pass the schema and solve the task at different costs or risks, the objective must move from imitation to preference comparison.

16.8 Trajectory Preference Optimization

Consider two independently collected continuations of the connection-pool repair that start from identical diagnostic context and both resolve the leak. One continuation runs the entire integration test suite after every minor edit, while the other runs targeted unit tests and executes the full suite only upon completion. Supervised imitation of both trajectories teaches the agent wasteful verification habits alongside disciplined ones; training only on the efficient path discards the signal that the slower trajectory was functionally correct yet needlessly expensive. A preference comparison captures this trade-off directly, steering the policy toward the efficient trajectory without treating the slower candidate as a failed repair. Not every pair of recorded interactions forms an admissible preference comparison: in the recovery demonstration, for instance, the malformed proposal e2 and the subsequent correction e4 cannot be paired as preferences, because the correction conditioned on environmental error messages that e2 never saw. Valid comparisons require alternative decisions evaluated under identical available information.

↩︎ The preference-pair admission predicates, which require competing trajectories to branch from identical conditioning contexts, are established in Admission predicates.

16.8.1 The trajectory preference loss

Let \(\ComparisonContext\) identify the shared comparison context and evaluation criterion, and let \(\PreferredTrajectory\) and \(\DispreferredTrajectory\) denote the preferred and dispreferred trajectory records. Unlike a supervised fine-tuning mask that zeros out dispreferred attempts, a preference comparison selects the divergent policy spans in both records and contrasts their log-probability ratios against a frozen starting checkpoint \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\) (typically the supervised fine-tuned checkpoint). These subscripts reflect the relative ranking assigned during curation rather than binary correctness, and the word “reference” now names a different role than it did for \(\pi^*\) in section 16.6. There, \(\pi^*\) was the expert whose actions defined correct behavior, whereas here \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\) is only the starting checkpoint the update is measured against. Both the active policy \(\Policy\) and \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\) must assign nonzero probability to all scored tokens under the specified input prefixes.

For each trajectory, let \(\InteractionHistory_t\) denote the rendered context prefix used to predict action token \(\SequenceToken_t\), and let \(\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\Trajectory)\) denote the set of policy-generated token indices selected by the preference objective. Define the reference-relative score as

\[\ReferenceRelativeScore(\Trajectory;\ComparisonContext)=\sum_{t\in\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\Trajectory)} \log\frac{\Policy(\SequenceToken_t\mid \InteractionHistory_t)}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(\SequenceToken_t\mid \InteractionHistory_t)}.\]

Environment observations interleaved within \(\InteractionHistory_t\) condition subsequent predictions but carry no policy likelihood terms. The token selection set \(\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\Trajectory)\) is customized specifically for each comparison. The dispreferred trajectory is evaluated solely to construct a negative contrast; it never serves as an imitation target.

Earlier reinforcement learning from human feedback pipelines fit a separate reward model to pairwise comparisons and then optimized the policy against that learned surrogate using reinforcement learning (Christiano et al. 2017; Ouyang et al. 2022). Direct preference optimization (DPO) bypasses the reward model entirely by optimizing a logistic loss directly over the difference of policy-to-reference log-probability ratios (Rafailov et al. 2023). Using the standard sigmoid function \(\SigmoidFunction(\PreferenceMargin)=1/(1+e^{-\PreferenceMargin})\), a positive preference scale \(\PreferenceScale\) that sets how strongly the objective resists drift from \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\), and a dataset \(\PreferencePairDataset\) of curated comparison pairs, the resulting objective over selected tokens is

\[\DpoLoss=-\mathbb E_{(\ComparisonContext,\PreferredTrajectory,\DispreferredTrajectory)\sim\PreferencePairDataset} \log\SigmoidFunction\!\left(\PreferenceScale[\ReferenceRelativeScore(\PreferredTrajectory;\ComparisonContext)-\ReferenceRelativeScore(\DispreferredTrajectory;\ComparisonContext)]\right). \tag{7}\]

The classic DPO derivation applies to single-turn responses generated for an identical prompt under the Bradley–Terry preference model6 (Bradley and Terry 1952) and penalizes drift from \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\) with a Kullback–Leibler divergence, the expected log-ratio of two distributions’ probabilities, which is zero only when they match. The penalty is taken in the “reverse” direction, with the expectation over the trained policy’s own outputs rather than the reference’s. In outline, the best policy under that penalty is the reference reweighted by the exponentiated reward and divided by a per-context normalizing term (the partition function) that makes its probabilities sum to one. Solving that relation for the reward expresses it as \(\PreferenceScale\) times the policy-to-reference log ratio plus \(\PreferenceScale\) times the logarithm of the normalizer. Because both members of a pair share the same context, they share the same normalizer, and it drops out when one score is subtracted from the other, which is all the Bradley–Terry model ever uses. Consequently, the implicit reward is identified only up to an additive constant per prompt context. Applying this framework to multi-step agent trajectories by scoring selected token spans \(\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\Trajectory)\) is an engineering surrogate built on that foundation, and closed-loop evaluation must validate it.

6 Bradley–Terry Model: The paired-comparison model of Bradley and Terry (1952) gives the probability that item \(\FirstComparedItem\) is preferred to item \(\SecondComparedItem\) as a logistic function of the difference of their latent scores. Only score differences are identified, which is why DPO’s implied reward carries an input-dependent constant that cancels within a pair, and why a DPO score has no meaning outside the comparison that produced it.

Bradley, Ralph Allan, and Milton E. Terry. 1952. “Rank Analysis of Incomplete Block Designs: I. The Method of Paired Comparisons.” Biometrika 39 (3/4): 324–45. https://doi.org/10.2307/2334029.

↩︎ The Kullback–Leibler divergence, used there to compare a baseline’s and a candidate’s tool-call distributions, is established in Offline comparison and shadow evidence.

The full probability of generating a multi-turn trajectory is the product of the policy’s probabilities on its action tokens and the environment’s transition probabilities on the interleaved observations. When the preference objective forms the ratio of a single recorded trajectory’s probability under \(\Policy\) to its probability under \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\), both the numerator and the denominator evaluate the same historical observations, so the stationary environment transition terms divide out and leave only the sum of action log-probability ratios in \(\ReferenceRelativeScore(\Trajectory;\ComparisonContext)\). Between two alternative trajectories \(\PreferredTrajectory\) and \(\DispreferredTrajectory\), however, the observed states diverge after the first different tool call. Those divergent observations alter the conditioning context \(\InteractionHistory_t\) for all downstream actions, ensuring that environmental feedback continues to shape the cumulative score difference even though the policy assigns no explicit probability to environment outputs.

For a single comparison pair, define the preference margin \(\PreferenceMargin=\PreferenceScale(\PreferredReferenceRelativeScore-\DispreferredReferenceRelativeScore)\) (a new use of \(\PreferenceMargin\), unrelated to the rationale tokens of section 16.4.4), where \(\PreferredReferenceRelativeScore\) and \(\DispreferredReferenceRelativeScore\) represent the reference-relative log-likelihood sums for the preferred and dispreferred trajectories. Differentiating the loss in equation 7 with respect to the policy parameters \(\ModelParameters\) while holding the reference policy fixed yields

\[\nabla_\ModelParameters\DpoLoss=-\PreferenceScale\SigmoidFunction(-\PreferenceMargin) \left(\sum_{t\in\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\PreferredTrajectory)}\nabla_\ModelParameters\log\Policy(\SequenceToken_t\mid \InteractionHistory_t) -\sum_{t\in\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\DispreferredTrajectory)}\nabla_\ModelParameters\log\Policy(\SequenceToken_t\mid \InteractionHistory_t)\right). \tag{8}\]

The gradient has three systems consequences. First, identical selected tokens sharing an identical conditioning prefix contribute equal gradient vectors to both sums in equation 8 and cancel exactly. A shared prefix therefore suffers no negative gradient updates simply because it also appears at the start of the dispreferred record. Once the paths diverge, identical tokens no longer cancel because their conditioning histories differ. Second, because shared model parameters parameterize all tokens, updating weights on selected positions also shifts likelihoods on unselected positions. Third, the full scalar coefficient multiplying the difference of gradient sums is \(\PreferenceScale\SigmoidFunction(-\PreferenceMargin)\); \(\SigmoidFunction(-\PreferenceMargin)=1-\SigmoidFunction(\PreferenceMargin)\) is its saturation factor. At the reference policy, both scores are zero (\(\PreferenceMargin=0\)), so the factor is \(0.5\) and the coefficient is \(\PreferenceScale/2\). At fixed \(\PreferenceScale\), increasing a positive margin reduces both quantities, dampening the contribution of pairs the policy already distinguishes. The coefficient alone does not determine the full gradient norm or the optimizer step, which also depend on the gradient vectors and optimization state.

Napkin Math 16.4: How hard one comparison pulls
Problem: After some training, the policy has moved relative to the reference on one admitted comparison. What are the pair’s margin, loss, saturation factor, and full gradient coefficient, and how do they change if \(\PreferenceScale\) doubles?

Variables: The selected-token log-probability sums are -8 for the active policy and -10 for the reference on the preferred record; the dispreferred record’s sums are -11 and -10. Use \(\PreferenceScale=\) 0.2. These are hypothetical, dimensionless natural-log quantities.

Math: The reference-relative scores are 2 and -1, giving margin \(\PreferenceMargin=\) 0.6, logistic score \(\SigmoidFunction(\PreferenceMargin)=\) 0.646, and pair loss 0.437, down from 0.693 at \(\PreferenceMargin=0\). The saturation factor \(\SigmoidFunction(-\PreferenceMargin)\) falls from 0.5 to 0.354, while the full coefficient \(\PreferenceScale\SigmoidFunction(-\PreferenceMargin)\) falls from 0.1 to 0.0709. At \(\PreferenceScale=\) 0.4, the same log-probabilities give \(\PreferenceMargin=\) 1.2, a logistic score of 0.769, a saturation factor of 0.231, and a full coefficient of 0.0926.

Systems insight: Changing \(\PreferenceScale\) changes both the margin and the gradient prefactor. In this fixed-record comparison, doubling it reduces the saturation factor from 0.354 to 0.231 but increases the full coefficient from 0.0709 to 0.0926. This local coefficient comparison establishes neither the eventual policy’s divergence from the reference nor the size of its optimizer step. The logistic score \(\SigmoidFunction(\PreferenceMargin)\) rises with \(\PreferenceScale\) for the same log-probabilities, reflecting the model’s fit to this comparison rather than the operational success probability of the repair. Furthermore, the margin can increase either by boosting the likelihood of the preferred trajectory or by suppressing the dispreferred trajectory; only closed-loop execution can verify whether the resulting policy generalizes or merely avoids the dispreferred path.

Scoring an entire trajectory spreads credit and blame across every divergent token in the interaction, whereas narrowing \(\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\Trajectory)\) to the branch point where the competing tool calls or edits first diverge focuses the gradient on that decision. Because log-probability ratios can be positive or negative, summing over longer sequences does not bias the margin in one direction, but it does increase score variance. Teams commonly test two modifications to control length sensitivity: dividing each score by its selected-token count \(|\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\Trajectory)|\), and adding an auxiliary supervised imitation loss on the preferred record \(\PreferredTrajectory\) to keep the policy from drifting into low-likelihood outputs. Both choices are validated on closed-loop trials.

16.8.2 Reference log-probability caching

Whichever token span \(\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\Trajectory)\) the objective scores, evaluating \(\ReferenceRelativeScore(\Trajectory;\ComparisonContext)\) online requires holding a frozen copy of \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\) in accelerator memory alongside the active policy and running reference forward passes on every preferred and dispreferred record. Because \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\) and the training dataset are fixed across a DPO run, the pipeline can precompute and cache the reference policy’s log-probabilities for all selected action tokens ahead of time. When token masks and aggregation rules are also fixed, storing only the scalar sum \(\ReferenceLogProbabilitySum(\Trajectory)\) avoids saving per-token arrays or materializing full-vocabulary logits. To keep cached sums from going silently stale across experiments, the cache key must bind every input that affects the number: the reference weights and any adapter configuration (section 16.10), the tokenizer and chat template, the exact token sequences and selected indices, the attention, position, and truncation settings, and the floating-point precision mode (such as FP16 vs. BF16). Changing data preprocessing or token selection invalidates cached sums even when the reference weights have not changed. Pipelines therefore check cached values against live recomputations on sampled batches within a declared numerical tolerance (for example, \(10^{-5}\)) rather than assuming bitwise identity across accelerators or kernels.

Caching drops the reference weights and their forward passes from the training loop, trading preprocessing compute, disk space, and host-to-device bandwidth for accelerator memory. That removes the reference model’s weight and activation footprint, while the active policy’s weights, optimizer state (the running moments an adaptive optimizer keeps for each trained parameter), gradient buffers, and forward activations remain on the device. Section 16.10 itemizes those remaining arrays. Preference optimization changes the loss function applied to a curated corpus, while on-policy labeling and fault injection change which states the corpus covers. When the bottleneck at serving time is instead the cost of carrying a long procedural prompt or running a large teacher model on every turn, policy distillation moves that behavior into a smaller student (section 16.9).

16.9 Policy Distillation

Consider an automated repair agent whose system prompt carries a detailed debugging checklist and worked examples showing how to inspect a stack trace before revising a tool call. In production, the agent resends this entire prompt prefix on every turn of every trajectory, consuming context-window capacity and raising time-to-first-token latency even though the procedural workflow belongs in the weights under table 1. Training the model to internalize the procedure lets the runtime drop the checklist from the prompt and reclaim those tokens on every call. Policy distillation transfers behavior from an expensive teacher configuration, whether a larger model family, a long procedural prompt, or a broader test-time search budget, into a cheaper student configuration, provided the teacher’s trajectories pass the same target admission filters as any other demonstration before entering training.

16.9.1 Context distillation

To move that debugging checklist out of the repair agent’s system prompt, the pipeline first samples candidate demonstrations under the expanded prompt containing the full checklist, diagnostic routines, or few-shot examples, and keeps only trajectories that pass the admission filters. Context distillation7 then strips the static procedural guidance from the prompt and trains the student to emit the same selected actions from the shortened prefix, without removing the dynamic evidence the policy still needs at serving time. Because this transformation intentionally changes the conditioning input rather than replaying the teacher’s exact prompt, the lineage record preserves both prompt versions and the transformation rule (section 16.3).

7 Context Distillation: Askell et al. (2021) introduced the term for training a model without a long prompt to match the output distribution the same model produced with it, motivated by context-window limits and the compute required to process prompt prefixes repeatedly.

Askell, Amanda, Yuntao Bai, Anna Chen, et al. 2021. “A General Language Assistant as a Laboratory for Alignment.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2112.00861, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2112.00861.

Stripping static procedural guidance differs from stripping dynamic environment feedback, such as an authorization refusal or a schema error that justified a corrective call. Removing causal observations leaves the corrected action unmotivated by its prefix and trains the student to guess state changes without evidence. When a recorded trace such as trace_r19_01_redacted_v1 is shortened for context distillation, the pipeline records a new derived artifact with its own target review while keeping the historical runtime verdict attached to the original long-prompt trace.

The division of responsibilities in table 1 decides both what may be distilled and when to retrain. Stable procedures can move into weights, whereas current task facts, tool schemas, and authorization rules must stay in the prompt so an interface update does not break the model. By the same token, any rule that changes faster than the team can curate data, run fine-tuning, and clear the release gate, such as a business policy updated weekly, behaves as a contract in practice and stays in context.

The distilled student is evaluated with the abbreviated production prompt it will actually receive at serving time, checking that task acceptance and tool-calling accuracy hold while prompt tokens fall. Because end-to-end latency also depends on KV-cache prefix reuse, output length, tool execution time, and recovery turns, latency savings must be measured on closed-loop runs rather than inferred from prompt token counts alone. A distilled policy that reads fewer input tokens but takes an extra tool round-trip to recover from a bad argument can lose the prefill saving immediately.

16.9.2 Teacher distillation

When the teacher is a stronger model family or a multi-sample search harness rather than a longer prompt on the same model, teacher distillation transfers its verified trajectories into a smaller student. In classical knowledge distillation, a student trains on the teacher’s full probability distribution over classes8 (Buciluă et al. 2006; Hinton et al. 2015). Because trajectory collectors that call a frontier model or search harness over an API receive discrete sampled tool calls and messages rather than full-vocabulary logits, agent teacher distillation works by filtered imitation on verified trajectories rather than soft-target matching.

8 Soft Targets: Buciluă et al. (2006) compressed large ensembles by labeling data with the ensemble’s predictions and training a small network on those labels; Hinton et al. (2015) named the method distillation and trained on the teacher’s temperature-softened probabilities over every class. Soft targets require the teacher’s logits; a collector that sees only sampled messages has one token per position instead of a distribution.

Buciluă, Cristian, Rich Caruana, and Alexandru Niculescu-Mizil. 2006. “Model Compression.” Proceedings of the 12th ACM SIGKDD International Conference on Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining (KDD), 535–41. https://doi.org/10.1145/1150402.1150464.
Hinton, Geoffrey, Oriol Vinyals, and Jeff Dean. 2015. “Distilling the Knowledge in a Neural Network.” arXiv Preprint.
Zelikman, Eric, Yuhuai Wu, Jesse Mu, and Noah D. Goodman. 2022. “STaR: Bootstrapping Reasoning with Reasoning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 15476–88.

Whether the teacher is a larger model, a test-time search loop (such as tree search or best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) sampling), or the student’s own base model run with a long prompt, its candidate rollouts must pass the consumer’s admission checks before training. Fluent reasoning or high self-reported confidence is never a substitute for verification. Training smaller models on verified trajectories from stronger teachers reliably improves tool-use accuracy and multi-step stability over zero-shot prompting (Chen et al. 2023; Zeng et al. 2023). The same filtered-imitation loop can even use the model’s own verified rollouts, as in STaR (Zelikman et al. 2022), subject to the self-selection narrowing risks of The Self-Improvement Loop. Because curation keeps only verified successes, the student trains on a cleaner distribution than the teacher’s raw rollouts and is not capped at the unguided teacher’s pass rate. However, it will also absorb any inefficient tool habits or biases in the teacher that slip past the admission filter.

Distillation settles where the training trajectories come from, independently of the loss objective and the parameter artifact (section 16.2.2). Whether the run updates every weight or a compact adapter, the learned behavior is only as good as the admitted demonstrations. What the artifact choice changes is the memory and storage cost of holding the update, especially when a deployment maintains several specialized agent roles.

16.10 Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning

Full fine-tuning of an agent policy updates and stores a complete replica of every base parameter, requiring the training system to allocate memory for base weights, gradients, and optimizer state across the entire model. For an adaptive-moment optimizer under mixed precision, that state is a higher-precision master copy of each weight plus two running moments, so each trained parameter carries several times the bytes of its weight. Training a second specialized agent role, such as a diagnostic planner or an execution monitor, duplicates that resource footprint in full. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning avoids this multiplicative overhead by freezing the base model and training only a compact set of auxiliary parameters known as an adapter9 (Houlsby et al. 2019), which is distributed alongside the immutable base checkpoint. For systems operating multiple specialized agent roles, lightweight adapters streamline storage and version management. Adapters give modularity, not isolation. An adapter is only a set of weight matrices; like the rest of the weights, it shapes proposals and holds no permissions of its own, and roles served from one process share that process’s failure domain (section 16.10.2).

9 Adapter Modules: Houlsby et al. (2019) introduced adapters as small bottleneck layers inserted inside each Transformer block, updating only those bottleneck parameters per task. However, inserting sequential layers introduces serial computational dependencies during inference. Low-rank adaptation avoids this serving latency by parameterizing updates to match the exact dimensions of existing weight matrices, enabling operators to fold the adapter directly into the base weights when serving without adding serial layer overhead.

Houlsby, Neil, Andrei Giurgiu, Stanislaw Jastrzebski, Bruna Morrone, Quentin de Laroussilhe, Andrea Gesmundo, Mohammad Attariyan, and Sylvain Gelly. 2019. “Parameter-Efficient Transfer Learning for NLP.” Proceedings of the 36th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 97: 2790–99.

Rather than inserting extra sequential layers into the network, low-rank adaptation (LoRA) expresses the weight update to a selected projection matrix through two low-rank factor matrices (Hu et al. 2021). Let \(\WeightMatrix_0\in\mathbb{R}^{\LayerOutputWidth\times \LayerInputWidth}\) denote a frozen base weight matrix acting on column-vector inputs. LoRA models the adapted matrix as

\[\WeightMatrix=\WeightMatrix_0+\frac{\AdapterScalingFactor}{\AdapterRank}\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix,\qquad \AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\in\mathbb{R}^{\LayerOutputWidth\times \AdapterRank},\quad \AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\in\mathbb{R}^{\AdapterRank\times \LayerInputWidth}.\]

Here, the rank hyperparameter \(\AdapterRank \ll \min(\LayerInputWidth, \LayerOutputWidth)\) bounds the rank of the update, and the scaling factor \(\AdapterScalingFactor/\AdapterRank\) stabilizes optimization across different choices of \(\AdapterRank\). The two factor matrices contain \(\AdapterRank(\LayerInputWidth+\LayerOutputWidth)\) trainable parameters. When sizing a run, engineers must sum this parameter count over all targeted matrices; attention projections and multi-layer perceptron projections frequently differ in dimensionality and need not be square. Target matrix selection and rank operate as primary capacity knobs, tuned directly against empirical validation performance.10

10 Low Intrinsic Rank: Hu and colleagues motivated LoRA by hypothesizing that weight updates during downstream adaptation lie on a manifold with low intrinsic dimension, showing that small ranks (\(\AdapterRank \in \{4, 8\}\)) matched full fine-tuning on standard language benchmarks (Hu et al. 2021). That empirical finding reflects the characteristics of those benchmark tasks rather than a universal theoretical guarantee. Fine-tuning an agent policy on novel tool invocation protocols or structured error recovery often demands specialized capacity, making systematic rank sweeps essential under the evaluation contract of this chapter.

Hu, Edward J., Yelong Shen, Phillip Wallis, Zeyuan Allen-Zhu, Yuanzhi Li, Shean Wang, Lu Wang, and Weizhu Chen. 2021. “LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2106.09685.

Initializing \(\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\) from a Gaussian distribution and setting \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix=0\) ensures that \(\FiniteChange\WeightMatrix = \frac{\AdapterScalingFactor}{\AdapterRank}\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\) is initially zero, allowing training to begin from the exact forward mapping of the pretrained base model. This asymmetric initialization is necessary to break symmetry without disturbing the initial forward pass. On the first backward step, the nonzero random entries in \(\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\) project the layer input into a nonzero gradient for \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\), whereas the gradient with respect to \(\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\) evaluates to zero because it is scaled by \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix=0\). Once the first optimizer step updates \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\) to nonzero values, subsequent backward passes propagate nonzero gradients into \(\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\) as well, whereas initializing both matrices to zero would leave both gradients at zero permanently. Freezing \(\WeightMatrix_0\) eliminates the memory required for base gradients and optimizer state, yet the backward pass still evaluates matrix-vector products through \(\WeightMatrix_0^T\) to propagate activation gradients down to earlier transformer layers. The resulting gradient with respect to the layer input combines contributions from both the frozen base path and the adapter bypass, as given in LoRA matrix decomposition.

16.10.1 Parameter-state inventory

Training 0.10 percent of the parameters still keeps 12.6 percent of the state.

Because \(\WeightMatrix_0\) participates in every forward and backward pass, freezing the base model does not shrink GPU memory in proportion to the trainable parameter count: training 0.1 percent of the parameters still keeps 100 percent of the frozen base weights in device memory. Engineers size training hardware by counting the bytes of each resident array (frozen base weights, adapter weights, gradients, master weights, and optimizer moments), as notebook 16.5 does for a full fine-tuning run versus a LoRA run under mixed precision. Dynamic activations and temporary kernel workspaces are budgeted separately, because their size scales with sequence length, batch layout, and attention implementation rather than parameter count.

Napkin Math 16.5: What adapter training removes from the inventory
Problem: A hypothetical model contains 8,000,000,000 base parameters. The run attaches rank-16 adapters to 2 matrices in each of 32 layers. Each selected matrix maps an input width of 4,096 to an output width of 4,096. No other parameters are trained in the adapter case. What parameter-state memory does this save compared with training every base parameter?

For each trainable parameter, assume a weight using 2 bytes, a gradient using 2 bytes, a separate higher-precision master weight that the optimizer updates, using 4 bytes, and two optimizer moments (running averages of the gradient and its square) each using 4 bytes. Frozen parameters retain only their 2 bytes weight copy. This is the mixed-precision accounting of adaptive-moment optimization (Kingma and Ba 2015; Micikevicius et al. 2018), totaling 16 bytes per trainable parameter as modeled by Rajbhandari et al. (2020); it is one declared implementation convention, not a requirement of every optimizer or framework. Count one unsharded resident copy of each array; display capacities in decimal MB and GB.

Math: Each adapted matrix adds \(\AdapterRank(\LayerInputWidth+\LayerOutputWidth)\) parameters, so the adapters contain 32 \(\times\) 2 \(\times\) 16 \(\times\) (4,096 \(+\) 4,096) \(=\) 8,388,608 parameters, 0.10 percent of the base. The convention assigns 16 bytes of state to each trainable parameter and 2 bytes to each frozen one. Table 10 inventories the resulting arrays.

Result: The adapter arrays total 134.22 MB. Including the frozen base gives approximately 16.13 GB, compared with 128 GB for full-parameter training under the same convention, a reduction of approximately 111.87 GB, or 7.9×. The saving comes from dropping gradients, master weights, and optimizer moments for the frozen base.

Systems insight: Even though adapter training updates only 0.10 percent of the parameters, the system must still allocate 12.6 percent of the full-parameter memory state. The frozen base weights remain resident throughout execution and constitute 99.2 percent of what remains. Consequently, total parameter-state savings are bounded by the ratio of trainable to frozen bytes per parameter (here 16 bytes against 2 bytes, so no choice of rank can push the reduction past that ratio). Rank adjustments exert virtually no influence on this footprint; doubling the rank increases memory by only 134.22 MB. The primary engineering lever for shrinking adapter memory state is therefore the numerical precision of the frozen base itself. Dynamic activations and execution workspaces, required under both training paradigms, must be provisioned on top of this subtotal.

Kingma, Diederik P., and Jimmy Ba. 2015. “Adam: A Method for Stochastic Optimization.” 3rd International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Beyond persistent parameter state, a complete memory plan must budget for forward activations (whether retained in high-bandwidth memory or selectively recomputed), temporary kernel scratchpads, collective communication buffers, allocator fragmentation, and operating headroom. Sharding and offloading distribute these resident arrays across cluster nodes or host memory tiers, trading network or PCIe interconnect bandwidth for device memory headroom. When on-device capacity is severely constrained, quantizing the frozen base weights directly targets the dominant state component, as demonstrated by QLoRA (quantized LoRA) (Dettmers et al. 2023), trading runtime dequantization arithmetic and metadata storage for lower memory occupancy. Because dynamic activation footprints and kernel workspaces scale with context length, batch structure, and attention kernels, engineers should profile these dynamic terms directly on the target hardware cluster before finalizing resource sizing.

16.10.2 Adapter release and compatibility

Once training finishes, the same split between the frozen base \(\WeightMatrix_0\) and the low-rank factors \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\) and \(\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\) governs how the artifact is shipped and served. Because those factors express weight deltas relative to a specific base matrix \(\WeightMatrix_0\), mounting a trained adapter onto a silently updated base checkpoint corrupts model outputs without raising a tensor-shape error. Releasing an adapter for a specialized agent role therefore requires shipping a configuration manifest alongside the low-rank weights that pins the parent base model version, target projection names, rank \(\AdapterRank\), scaling factor \(\AdapterScalingFactor\), tokenizer revision, prompt templates, numerical precision, and links to the training corpus and evaluation audit. At serving time, the runtime must also enforce KV-cache prefix compatibility: cached prefix states computed under one adapter cannot be reused by another adapter that modifies the layers that produced them.

↩︎ The rule that cached key-value prefix state is reusable only under the same base weights and active adapters is established in Prefix compatibility.

Sheng, Ying, Shiyi Cao, Dacheng Li, Coleman Hooper, Shuo Chen, Daxin Jiang, Christopher Rodriguez, et al. 2024. “S-LoRA: Serving Thousands of Concurrent LoRA Adapters.” Proceedings of the 7th Conference on Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys).
Chen, Liyuan, Zihao Ye, Yonghao Wu, Danyang Zhuo, Luis Ceze, and Arvind Krishnamurthy. 2024. “Punica: Multi-Tenant LoRA Serving.” Proceedings of the 7th Conference on Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys).

Keeping adapters decoupled from the base weights lets a serving engine host several agent roles over one shared base model in accelerator memory, batching concurrent requests across distinct adapters while running the shared base projections in common matrix multiplications (Sheng et al. 2024; Chen et al. 2024). While this layout amortizes base-weight memory across roles, each role still pays for its own factor matrices and dynamic activations. Sharing one serving process across roles also provides no fault or authority isolation between them. A bad adapter or anomalous input shares the process’s resource limits, and distinct roles still need distinct runtime capability grants. When a deployment serves only a single role, folding the adapter directly into the base weights (\(\WeightMatrix = \WeightMatrix_0 + \frac{\AdapterScalingFactor}{\AdapterRank}\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\)) removes per-token adapter kernel overhead and yields a single dense checkpoint.

If a newly deployed adapter regresses in production, rolling back the routing configuration sends new task admissions back to the prior verified release. Model rollback, however, cannot undo tool side effects already committed to external systems (database writes, network calls, or file edits) or settle in-flight trajectories on its own. The release policy must therefore declare whether active runs finish under their starting adapter or hand over to the fallback policy under the runtime’s recovery rules. Whether an update is packaged as an adapter or a dense checkpoint, deciding if it is safe to promote requires closed-loop evaluation.

16.11 Evaluating a Fine-Tuned Agent

A fine-tuned checkpoint can reach a low loss on a repair corpus yet fail to resolve actual connection-pool leaks in production. Training configurations and data manifests record how an artifact was produced, but only closed-loop trials reveal how the policy navigates multi-step execution when its own actions dictate future observations. The task acceptance contract defined in The Task Acceptance Contract governs these trials, addressing the specific failure modes and behavioral shifts that weight updates introduce.

16.11.1 Comparable loss and closed-loop performance

Held-out token loss measures prediction accuracy under teacher forcing, where recorded prefixes are supplied at every step, whereas closed-loop task completion runs the policy on the context history its own actions create under a fixed time and token budget. Even as an offline check for optimization bugs, token losses from two training runs are comparable only when computed under an identical measurement contract: if one run uses an all-token loss and another masks observations, their dashboard losses average over different token sets. In notebook 16.1, an unmasked causal loss averages across 16,383 prediction terms (of which read-only observations make up 77.1 percent of the stored input), whereas the masked objective averages over the 800 selected response tokens. Comparing raw training losses across those two runs rewards the unmasked checkpoint for predicting repetitive compiler boilerplate rather than choosing better actions. Valid offline comparison requires recomputing held-out loss on a common validation split under the exact same prompt contexts, tokenizer, serialization template, target mask, and normalization rule, logging both the active-target count and the split version. If two checkpoints use different tokenizers, their per-token losses do not even share a common unit.

Example 16.1: A lower loss is not yet a release decision
Scenario: In an evaluation bake-off, two candidate checkpoints are trained on the same curated trajectories: one with an all-token loss and the other with a selected-target response mask. The run trained on all tokens displays a substantially lower dashboard loss.

Diagnosis: The two training metrics calculate averages over incompatible target sets. The all-token run lowers its average by predicting the repetitive parts of environment observations (echoed schemas, boilerplate, repeated paths), whereas the masked run evaluates only policy tokens. Evaluating both checkpoints on an identical held-out validation set using matched response masks makes their loss values comparable. Even a checkpoint with superior held-out loss, however, is evaluated solely on teacher-forced prefixes; during autonomous rollouts, compounding errors can still cause it to invoke invalid tools, misinterpret unexpected exceptions, or blow past its step budget.

Systems lesson: Rank candidate checkpoints offline using an identical held-out evaluation loss, but base promotion and release decisions exclusively on closed-loop task acceptance. A checkpoint boasting lower loss remains unpromotable if it fails the recovery criteria specified in the release contract. When modifying training data and loss masks simultaneously, run controlled ablation trials to isolate the source of any observed performance shift.

16.11.2 Task and behavior measurements

Because neither held-out loss nor a single syntax check can show whether a weight update improved repair behavior without breaking other tasks, the release harness evaluates a candidate against the full policy stack it would replace across five complementary measurements: budgeted task acceptance, call validity, schema robustness, resource use, and capability retention. Budgeted task acceptance is the primary release outcome, measured under the declared criteria, runtime feedback rules, pinned fixtures, and resource envelopes of the task acceptance contract. Every initiated trial is counted, so runs that stall or time out at the observation cutoff are recorded as failures or unresolved verdicts rather than dropped. When the production deployment wraps the model in a multi-candidate selection or repair loop, the harness evaluates that composite loop alongside diagnostic views such as single-attempt pass rate (pass@1) and repeated-run consistency (pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\), Potential and repeated-run consistency).

When a trial fails that acceptance check, an end-to-end pass rate does not show whether the policy chose a flawed repair plan or failed to form a legal tool call in the first place. Measuring call validity separates those causes by checking whether generated tool calls satisfy the active interface contract in the production parser and schema validator. Rather than collapsing every bad call into a single failure rate, the harness separates unparsable outputs, missing required fields, type mismatches, and truncated proposals from downstream tool execution errors (table 11).

Passing the validator on familiar tool definitions still does not show whether the fine-tuned policy is reading the delivered contract or recalling argument names from its weights. Evaluating schema robustness tests whether the policy generalizes to modified tool interfaces, scoring familiar schemas separately from renamed schemas and unseen tool families (Qin et al. 2024) with mock tools and call-site checks updated in lockstep (section 16.7). Over \(\ScoredToolCallCount\) proposed calls \(\ScoredToolCall_i\), each checked against its active schema set \(\ActiveSchemaSet_i\), schema-following accuracy is the fraction of calls that name a declared tool and satisfy its type schema:

Qin, Yujia, Shihao Liang, Yining Ye, Kunlun Zhu, Lan Yan, Yaxi Lu, Yankai Lin, et al. 2024. “ToolLLM: Facilitating Large Language Models to Master 16000+ Real-World APIs.” Proceedings of the 12th International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

\[\text{SFA}=\frac{1}{\ScoredToolCallCount}\sum_{i=1}^{\ScoredToolCallCount}\EventIndicator\{\operatorname{tool}(\ScoredToolCall_i)\in\ActiveSchemaSet_i\ \wedge\ \ScoredToolCall_i\models\ActiveSchemaSet_i\}.\]

On the same evaluated calls, parametric recall error is the fraction of calls that emit parameter names from the training key set \(\TrainingSchemaKeySet\) that do not appear in the delivered schema \(\ActiveSchemaSet_i\):

\[\text{PRE}=\frac{1}{\ScoredToolCallCount}\sum_{i=1}^{\ScoredToolCallCount}\EventIndicator\{\exists \ToolArgumentKey\in\operatorname{keys}(\ScoredToolCall_i):\ToolArgumentKey\in\TrainingSchemaKeySet\wedge \ToolArgumentKey\notin\operatorname{params}(\ActiveSchemaSet_i)\}.\]

When an adapted policy’s SFA rises on familiar schemas while its PRE climbs on renamed schemas, the weights are recalling training-set argument keys rather than reading the prompt. Before blaming the weights, however, the release audit checks the serving prompt to confirm that the old key was not accidentally left in the template.

A candidate that passes both task and schema checks can still spend too many turns or tokens per repair to be viable in production. Accounting for resource use prices what the policy spends to achieve that acceptance rate, tracking wall-clock latency, turn counts, generated tokens, and API charges through the effective cost per accepted task \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) (equation 1.2), which divides the total spend across all initiated attempts (including rejected calls, retries, verifier runs, and sandbox cleanup) by the number of accepted solutions. Because \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) divides by the acceptance rate, a fine-tuned candidate that takes an extra diagnostic turn per attempt can still lower \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) if those turns prevent failed runs. If a candidate completes zero tasks in a trial set, the report lists total spend beside the zero-acceptance count rather than an undefined ratio.

↩︎ Comparing policies under one fixed resource envelope, with time and tokens reported separately from monetary charges, is established in Matched-budget comparison.

11 Catastrophic Forgetting: McCloskey and Cohen (1989) showed that a connectionist network trained sequentially on a new task can lose an earlier one almost entirely, because both share the same weights. A repair-trained policy shares its weights with every other behavior the agent still needs, so retention is measured on the release’s required task set rather than assumed from a narrow training corpus.

McCloskey, Michael, and Neal J. Cohen. 1989. “Catastrophic Interference in Connectionist Networks: The Sequential Learning Problem.” Psychology of Learning and Motivation 24: 109–65. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0079-7421(08)60536-8.

Even a repair update that improves acceptance at lower \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) can degrade the other tasks the deployment serves if weight updates overwrite broader skills. Testing capability retention checks that specializing the weights on repair traces has not degraded those non-repair tasks, using a regression suite drawn from the agent’s full operational scope rather than generic language benchmarks alone. When performance drops on non-repair tasks, engineers check both the serving template and the training mixture before attributing the drop to catastrophic forgetting11 (McCloskey and Cohen 1989), and re-evaluate any mitigation, such as mixing in general trajectories, lowering the learning rate, or switching to a LoRA adapter, on the same five-part scorecard.

16.11.3 Controlled comparison and conditional release

To attribute changes on those five measurements to the weight update rather than to a coincidental prompt or schema edit, the release protocol varies one factor at a time under the fixture contract of Task fixtures. Each comparison pins task fixtures, acceptance criteria, resource budgets, and random seeds across repeated trials, binding every score to an artifact manifest that records the checkpoint or adapter hash, base model hash, tokenizer version, serialization template, decoding parameters, and harness commit.

The leakage controls of Evaluation leakage govern contamination here as well. Re-prompted attempts, redacted traces, and paraphrased variants inherit their source task’s split. Checkpoints and tuning mixtures are selected on validation splits alone. A test suite whose failures were fed back into training or prompt tuning has become development data and must be replaced by a fresh split. Benchmarking on familiar repositories or APIs reflects legitimate deployment requirements, but a claim that the fine-tuned policy generalizes demands held-out schema and environment families.

Controlled ablations compare the fine-tuned checkpoint against the incumbent policy, as well as against the prompt-engineering, schema-refactoring, and runtime-guardrail alternatives prioritized by the intervention ladder. Because the candidate and the incumbent can run on the same tasks, the comparison is a paired one, and the paired-outcome discipline (an interval on the difference, never two overlapping single-arm intervals) applies unchanged. Reading several ablations at once adds one correction beyond a single pair. If each comparison is tested at the same significance level, the chance that at least one of them shows a false win grows with their number, so the per-comparison level must be tightened. Dividing it by the number of comparisons is the simplest such rule. A release claim resting on one seed or one favorable run is a sample of one.

↩︎ Paired comparison of two configurations on the same tasks, with intervals on the difference and the rule for splitting trials between new tasks and repeats, is established in Statistical Evaluation Rigor.

While a lower common validation loss confirms that training optimized the intended objective, only closed-loop task acceptance, recovery resilience, and load-tested serving configurations provide defensible release evidence. A fine-tuned candidate enters the same declared release gate as any other change (The declared decision rule), approved for an exact artifact hash and operating envelope under principle 12. Even after a fine-tuned model passes release qualification, external safety boundaries remain non-negotiable: runtime schema validators, permission sandboxes, token budgets, and supervisory recovery monitors must remain active in production. Assembling the scope, serialization, masking, batching, coverage, and release decisions into one auditable training manifest turns an admitted recovery record into a deployable policy candidate.

16.12 Case Study: Training the Repair Policy

The recovery record that opened the chapter can now become a training artifact. The behavior to teach is narrow. When the runtime refuses an invalid tool proposal, the policy should inspect the schema, issue a corrected invocation that satisfies the interface, and end with a bounded report. Consider an illustrative supervised fine-tuning run that ingests repair_demonstrations_v1 to produce candidate policy trained_policy_v1. That artifact is a set of weights, distinct from ws_r20, the workspace the demonstration produced.

Each of the three adaptation choices is settled by evidence the chapter has already developed. The objective is imitation because the record supplies a demonstrated correction, not an admissible comparison; e2 and e4 were produced under different information, so they cannot form a preference pair (section 16.8). The behavior source is the admitted recovery corpus. It covers the malformed-argument refusal the recording happened to contain, not the version conflict that section 16.6 showed a policy trained on it would meet. That uncovered state is therefore the first candidate for a labeling round or a fault-injected record, and the first evaluation of trained_policy_v1 must include trials that reach it. The artifact is a full-parameter update, chosen for two reasons the chapter has developed. The repair policy is a single role, so none of the multi-role sharing that makes adapters attractive applies yet. The lesson is also a new recovery behavior, the kind for which a small rank is not known in advance to suffice, so the full update serves as the reference that any later adapter candidate must match. The choice has a price. On the hypothetical model of notebook 16.5, it keeps 128 GB of parameter state where an adapter run would keep 16.13 GB. A rank sweep whose adapter matches the full update’s paired acceptance, or a second role that must share the base, would flip the decision toward the adapter, which would then be a different candidate with its own evidence. Changing any one of the three choices produces a configuration that needs its own evidentiary trail.

Table 12 details the formal manifest bindings for this candidate policy. In production workflows, recording version identifiers alone is insufficient; a complete training record must capture the exact artifact contents, serialization parameters, and optimization settings required to reproduce and audit the resulting model.

The mask and the serialization follow directly from the earlier sections. target_mask_v1 selects the policy-response spans of e3 (the schema query), e4 (the corrected tool call), and e6 (the final report), each with its authentic end-of-turn token. The malformed proposal and its pre-dispatch refusal at e2, the schema returned to e3, and the check summary at e5 carry \(\PositionLossMask_{t}=0\); they stay in the prefix as conditioning and receive no direct loss (table 2). Selecting e2 would teach the malformed call, and selecting the returned schema or e5 would teach the policy to write tool results instead of requesting them. Whether serialization_v1 may use one concatenated transcript or must serialize each selected invocation separately depends on whether the runtime compacted history between e2 and e6. If it did not, each selected response’s prefix is a prefix of one transcript and a single example is faithful. The manifest records which case held, from the delivered-input manifests, rather than assuming it, because a record that crossed the watermark would need one example per invocation at up to \((\TurnHorizon+1)/2\) the stored tokens (section 16.3.3). If the run packs these examples with others, block-diagonal attention and per-example label shifts keep each example’s context its own (section 16.5).

The release gate for trained_policy_v1 follows from the lesson it was meant to learn. Lower held-out loss on the repair corpus shows only that the objective was optimized. Promotion needs budgeted task acceptance on held-out repair tasks under the same runtime and fixtures as the incumbent, compared as paired trials, with \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) reported for both so that a slower but more reliable candidate is credited correctly. Because the lesson is “consult the delivered schema,” the most direct test is schema-following accuracy and parametric recall error on a renamed-argument set: a candidate whose PRE rises there may be relying on the recorded interface rather than the delivered schema. The gate also needs trials that reach the version-conflict state the corpus does not cover, and a retention suite for the agent’s other required tasks. A candidate missing any of these measurements is held, however its loss compares (section 16.11).

Even when the evaluations justify a staged release, the proposal boundary stays where it was. Fine-tuning makes the policy’s proposals more reliable without making the model an execution authority. The runtime keeps enforcing schema validation, least-privilege tool permissions, process isolation, and hard timeout budgets, and the agent’s final report remains a claim the harness checks against workspace artifacts. The manifest is what turns one admitted recovery into a reproducible, auditable policy update; the runtime is what keeps that update’s mistakes bounded.

16.13 Fallacies and Pitfalls

A fine-tuning pipeline can optimize its nominal loss objective with complete numerical fidelity while departing from the intended operational behavior. In agent systems, where training corpora interweave multi-turn plans, tool invocations, and environment feedback, subtle flaws emerge at the interfaces between admitted evidence, context assembly, loss masking, and deployment evaluation. Each of the following mistakes produces a concrete failure mode, and each carries a quantifiable engineering penalty illustrated in this chapter’s worked examples.

Fallacy: Every model response in an accepted trajectory is a desirable training target.

When an agent successfully recovers from an error, the recorded trajectory necessarily contains the initial flawed or refused proposal alongside the eventual correction. That initial misstep is essential as conditioning context, because it explains why the correction was required, but it is the one action the policy must never learn to emit. In the inventory of notebook 16.1, the refused positions number 160 tokens, 20 percent as many as the 800 selected response targets. An unselective mask over all assistant turns therefore puts 160 of the 960 response positions to work reproducing the malformed call. That share is a minority of the response weight, yet all of it rewards the refused proposal directly, in every recovery record that contains one. Systems must instead implement target admission masks (section 16.4): retain rejected proposals and environment observations in the prefix context so the agent learns to condition on failure signals, but restrict direct imitation loss to admitted response targets.

Pitfall: Treating efficient batching as a change with no learning consequences.

Packing multiple variable-length trajectories into a single contiguous buffer raises occupancy, but it alters training dynamics unless the execution kernel accounts for sequence boundaries. In notebook 16.2, sequence packing raises token occupancy from 19.1 percent to 76.6 percent. However, applying a standard causal attention mask across that packed buffer allows subsequent examples to attend across episode boundaries to unrelated earlier sequences. Loss normalization alters optimization just as profoundly: standard token-level averaging gives the long example 32× the short one’s weight, whereas per-example averaging weights both examples equally. Always configure block-diagonal attention with per-example position coordinates and reset label shifts (section 16.5), explicitly declare loss reduction semantics, and verify real execution throughput on target accelerator kernels before claiming a speedup.

Fallacy: Recovery examples and schema perturbations make a fine-tuned agent robust to the states it will meet.

Synthesized recovery trajectories and perturbed schemas broaden training coverage, but they do not provide a blanket guarantee against distribution shift. Theoretical bounds on policy regret depend on where state error rates are evaluated. Over an execution horizon of 30 steps with a 2 percent per-step disagreement rate, theorem 16.1 bounds excess cost at 18 units on reference demonstration states, but at 0.6 units on states generated by the learner itself. Furthermore, that tighter bound holds only while the disadvantage of each individual deviation remains near one, a condition that fails as soon as one unexpected tool error can spoil the rest of the task. Robust agent systems require sampling and labeling states actually reached by the learner, maintaining end-to-end coherence between schema transformations, tool calls, and parser observations (section 16.7), and stress-testing the policy under held-out operational shifts.

Pitfall: Reading a preference pair’s logistic score as the probability that the preferred repair succeeds.

The implicit reward margin computed in direct preference optimization reflects pairwise discrimination on historical data, not absolute operational success. In notebook 16.4, identical trajectory log-probabilities yield a logistic score of 0.646 at \(\PreferenceScale=\) 0.2, yet that score rises to 0.769 when the preference scale doubles to \(\PreferenceScale=\) 0.4. A metric that shifts this much with an optimization hyperparameter measures relative fitting margin rather than task competence. Furthermore, that margin can widen because the objective suppresses the dispreferred trajectory rather than improving the execution likelihood of the preferred one. Reliable acceptance rates cannot be read off training curves; they must be measured directly through closed-loop environment trials (section 16.11).

Fallacy: Training a small fraction of the parameters shrinks training memory in proportion.

Parameter-efficient fine-tuning cuts the number of updated weights to a small fraction, but accelerator memory does not scale down linearly with the parameter fraction. In notebook 16.5, low-rank adapters account for 0.10 percent of total model parameters, yet the training job still requires 12.6 percent of the parameter-associated memory consumed by a full fine-tuning run. This 7.9× reduction is governed by bytes per parameter across base and adapter states rather than the parameter count alone. The frozen base weights must remain resident in memory throughout forward and backward passes, accounting for 99.2 percent of what remains, while dynamic activation buffers and workspace memory scale with sequence length for both approaches. Dimension adapter training hardware by accounting for frozen base representations and empirically measured activation peaks (section 16.10).

Pitfall: Promoting the artifact with the lowest recorded loss.

Selecting an agent checkpoint based purely on validation loss conflates prefix prediction with closed-loop execution competence. In notebook 16.1, an unmasked causal evaluation averages across 16,383 prediction terms per example. Tool outputs that the policy never emits occupy 77.1 percent of the stored input. In contrast, a selective objective evaluates only the 800 target positions where the model generates actions. A checkpoint with lower overall loss may simply fit the repetitive parts of that environment text more tightly without improving decision quality. Even when measured exclusively on action tokens, offline likelihood improvements do not guarantee that the policy will avoid deadlocks or handle schema variations during execution. Always qualify production candidates by measuring scoped task acceptance, effective cost \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), schema compliance, and retained foundational capabilities (section 16.11), while maintaining strict runtime validation checks regardless of training performance.

16.14 Summary

Trajectory fine-tuning changes the policy itself, and every decision in this chapter serves one requirement, that the update teach the lesson curation admitted and nothing else. A trajectory mixes the policy’s responses with the environment’s replies, so a loss computed over the wrong positions, under the wrong prefixes, or with the wrong batch weighting can fall while the behavior it teaches drifts. Even a faithful update is fitted on recorded prefixes. Its value is settled only by closed-loop evidence on the states the policy reaches on its own, under the same runtime that bounds every proposal it makes.

The quantitative decisions of the chapter rest on the relations collected in table 13. Each row pairs a formal relation with the training, packaging, or release choice it governs and points back to the section where it is derived.

Key Takeaways: Preserve the lesson from record to release
  • Weights hold procedure, never contracts: Training changes what the policy proposes, not what the runtime permits. Stable procedure can move into the weights, while schemas, permissions, and any guidance that changes faster than a training and evaluation cycle stay in context, and enforcement stays in the runtime.
  • A trajectory has two authors; train one: Observations stay in the input as context but receive no direct loss. An unmasked average dilutes each selected target by the inverse of its selected share of predicted positions, 20.5× in the worked inventory, and teaches the policy to invent tool results.
  • A loader can change the lesson without changing the loss: Later evidence in a prefix, a shifted label, or a causal mask across packed examples each redefines the prediction problem. Normalization is a choice too, since token averaging gives a long trajectory 32× the weight of a short one in the worked batch.
  • Where error is measured sets the horizon penalty: The same 2 percent disagreement over 30 steps bounds excess cost at 18 units on reference states and 0.6 on learner states when the deviation disadvantage is bounded by one. Long-horizon agents therefore need labels on the states they actually visit.
  • A preference score measures one comparison: A pair’s logistic score moves with \(\PreferenceScale\) and can rise by suppressing the dispreferred record, so it measures fit to that comparison, never the probability that a repair is accepted.
  • Adapters cut state by bytes, not parameter fraction: Training 0.10 percent of the parameters still keeps 12.6 percent of the parameter state, because the frozen base stays resident. The lever is the frozen base’s representation, not the adapter rank.
  • Release on closed-loop acceptance, not loss: A candidate is promoted on task acceptance, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), schema behavior, and retained capability under its full serving configuration. Rollback selects an earlier policy for future work and leaves effects already dispatched to settlement and compensation.

Verified trajectory post-training (principle 13) now has a concrete implementation obligation at the token level: preserve useful conditioning while aligning the objective with the selected predictions, rather than equating every recorded policy token with a target. At the record level, it connects the admitted lesson to a versioned update whose usefulness remains an evaluation question. Invariant closure (principle 1) stays outside that update, which is why the proposal boundary holds from the first training example to the release decision.

16.15 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The core mechanism of this chapter is older than language models. Williams and Zipser named teacher forcing in 1989, training recurrent networks by feeding the recorded target back as the next input (Williams and Zipser 1989). At about the same time, ALVINN learned road following as a supervised mapping from camera images to a steering direction (Pomerleau 1988), the template for what control researchers came to call behavioral cloning. Both generations met the same obstacle. A policy trained on recorded prefixes is run on its own, and its first error moves it into inputs the recording never showed. Ross and Bagnell turned that obstacle into a quadratic bound in 2010 (Ross and Bagnell 2010), and DAgger answered it the following year by labeling the states the learner itself visits (Ross et al. 2011). Sequence-generation research reached the same diagnosis independently, with scheduled sampling (Bengio et al. 2015) and the name exposure bias (Ranzato et al. 2016).

Pomerleau, Dean A. 1988. “ALVINN: An Autonomous Land Vehicle in a Neural Network.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NIPS) 1: 305–13.
Bengio, Samy, Oriol Vinyals, Navdeep Jaitly, and Noam Shazeer. 2015. “Scheduled Sampling for Sequence Prediction with Recurrent Neural Networks.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2015) 28.
Christiano, P. F., J. Leike, T. B. Brown, M. Martic, S. Legg, and D. Amodei. 2017. “Deep Reinforcement Learning from Human Preferences.” In Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 30: Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems 2017, December 4-9, 2017, Long Beach, CA, USA, edited by Isabelle Guyon, Ulrike von Luxburg, Samy Bengio, Hanna M. Wallach, Rob Fergus, S. V. N. Vishwanathan, and Roman Garnett. Curran Associates.

Preference learning has a statistical root. Bradley and Terry’s 1952 model of paired comparisons (Bradley and Terry 1952) underlies the reward models that Christiano and colleagues fit to human comparisons of agent behavior (Christiano et al. 2017), and InstructGPT combined supervised demonstrations with such a reward model to align a language model (Ouyang et al. 2022). DPO then showed that the separate reward model and reinforcement learning stage could be folded into a classification loss on policy/reference log ratios (Rafailov et al. 2023), the form this chapter extends from responses to selected trajectory spans.

The cost of adaptation has its own lineage. Model compression (Buciluă et al. 2006) and knowledge distillation (Hinton et al. 2015) made a large model’s behavior a training signal for a cheaper one, and context distillation applied the same idea to a prompt (Askell et al. 2021). Adapter modules (Houlsby et al. 2019), low-rank adaptation (Hu et al. 2021), and quantized frozen bases (Dettmers et al. 2023) shrank what each adaptation stores and trains, while the systems accounting of mixed-precision training (Micikevicius et al. 2018) and ZeRO (Rajbhandari et al. 2020) made the per-parameter memory of that training explicit. Agent fine-tuning joined these threads once models emitted tool calls inside interleaved reasoning and action traces (Yao et al. 2023; Schick et al. 2023), and trajectories generated by stronger models became training data for smaller ones (Chen et al. 2023; Zeng et al. 2023). What changes for agents is the environment: a trajectory has two authors, which is why masking, serialization, and on-policy coverage become engineering obligations rather than details.

Dettmers, Tim, Artidoro Pagnoni, Ari Holtzman, and Luke Zettlemoyer. 2023. “QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 10088–115.
Micikevicius, Paulius, Sharan Narang, Jonah Alben, Gregory Diamos, Erich Elsen, David Garcia, Boris Ginsburg, et al. 2018. “Mixed Precision Training.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Yao, Shunyu, Jeffrey Zhao, Dian Yu, Nan Du, Izhak Shafran, Karthik Narasimhan, and Yuan Cao. 2023. “ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Schick, Timo, Jane Dwivedi-Yu, Roberto Dessı̀, Roberta Raileanu, Maria Lomeli, Luke Zettlemoyer, Nicola Cancedda, and Thomas Scialom. 2023. “Toolformer: Language Models Can Teach Themselves to Use Tools.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 68539–51.
Chen, Baian, Chang Shu, Ehsan Shareghi, Nigel Collier, Karthik Narasimhan, and Shunyu Yao. 2023. “FireAct: Toward Language Agent Fine-Tuning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2310.05915, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2310.05915.
Zeng, Aohan, Mingdao Liu, Rui Lu, Bowen Wang, Xiao Liu, Yuxiao Dong, and Jie Tang. 2023. “AgentTuning: Enabling Generalized Agent Capabilities for LLMs.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2310.12823, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2310.12823.

For further reading, six works trace this lineage from behavioral cloning to modern trajectory post-training:

  • Ross, Gordon, and Bagnell’s DAgger paper (Ross et al. 2011) states both regret bounds of theorem 16.1 and the reduction to online learning behind them; it is the shortest route to seeing why where error is measured matters more than how small it is.
  • Rafailov and colleagues’ DPO paper (Rafailov et al. 2023) derives the preference loss from a reference-regularized objective, and reading the derivation shows exactly which assumptions a trajectory-span surrogate relaxes.
  • Ouyang and colleagues’ InstructGPT paper (Ouyang et al. 2022) documents a complete demonstration-then-preference pipeline, including the human evaluations that compared the model after each stage.
  • Hu and colleagues’ LoRA paper (Hu et al. 2021) motivates low-rank updates and reports experiments on which matrices to adapt and at what rank, the knobs this chapter says to set by evaluation.
  • Rajbhandari and colleagues’ ZeRO paper (Rajbhandari et al. 2020) itemizes training memory by owner and precision, the same inventory discipline notebook 16.5 applies to an adapter run.
  • Krell and colleagues’ packing paper (Krell et al. 2021) treats packing as an equivalence to prove rather than a throughput trick, the stance the chapter’s packed-versus-separate logit test takes.
Rafailov, Rafael, Archit Sharma, Eric Mitchell, Stefano Ermon, Christopher D. Manning, and Chelsea Finn. 2023. “Direct Preference Optimization: Your Language Model Is Secretly a Reward Model.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 53728–41. https://doi.org/10.52202/075280-2338.
Ouyang, L., J. Wu, X. Jiang, D. Almeida, C. L. Wainwright, P. Mishkin, C. Zhang, et al. 2022. “Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 27730–44. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-2011.
Rajbhandari, Samyam, Jeff Rasley, Olatunji Ruwase, and Yuxiong He. 2020. “ZeRO: Memory Optimizations Toward Training Trillion Parameter Models.” SC20: International Conference for High Performance Computing, Networking, Storage and Analysis, 1–16. https://doi.org/10.1109/sc41405.2020.00024.

16.16 Exercises

Every given below is a hypothetical problem input chosen for round arithmetic, not a measurement of any system. Each problem asks for a calculation and the training or release decision it supports.

  1. Per-invocation serialization under a token budget (Basic). The serving policy of the repair agent omits earlier generated text before each later invocation, so every turn of a trajectory becomes its own training example (section 16.3). Assume that the delivered prefix at each turn still grows by approximately \(\TurnAppendLength\) tokens despite the omitted generated text. Trajectories run \(\TurnHorizon=40\) turns, each turn adds about \(\TurnAppendLength=500\) tokens, the corpus holds 2,000 admitted trajectories, and the run may store at most 200 million training tokens.

    1. Compute the stored tokens per trajectory under per-invocation serialization and under a single transcript, and the expansion factor between them.
    2. Compute how many complete trajectories fit the budget under each serialization, and what fraction of the corpus the per-invocation run can use.
    3. Name the serving-side change that would make a single transcript a faithful example, and state what the training pipeline must verify before relying on it.

  2. Target count and dilution (Basic). A serialized repair example stores 2,000 prompt and schema tokens, 18,000 observation tokens, and 1,500 policy-response tokens. Of the response tokens, 300 belong to a refused proposal that curation retains as context only. The prompt and schema count includes the initial boundary; no preceding external seed is supplied. The example has no rationale and no padding.

    1. Compute \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount\) under equation 1, the selected share of the stored input, and the factor by which an unmasked token average would dilute each selected target’s coefficient.
    2. A loader instead selects every policy-response token. Compute the fraction of the imitation weight that lands on the refused proposal, and state what behavior that weight trains.
    3. A colleague expects masking to cut this example’s training compute by the dilution factor from part (a). Explain why the saving is far smaller, and identify the only work a loader can skip at masked positions.

  3. Normalization and segmentation (Basic). A batch holds one long repair trajectory with 6,000 selected targets and 15 short file-lookup examples with 200 selected targets each.

    1. Compute the long trajectory’s share of the batch objective under token averaging (equation 2) and under example averaging (equation 3).
    2. Serialization instead splits the long trajectory into 20 per-invocation examples of 300 selected targets each, every example selecting only its own turn’s response. Recompute both shares.
    3. The short lookups exercise a capability the release must retain. Choose a normalization and a segmentation, and state what the choice costs the long task’s influence on the update.

  4. Packing and the cross-example work a kernel can skip (Intermediate). Ten independent examples have stored lengths of 16,384, 8,192, 6,144, 4,096, 4,096, 2,048, 2,048, 1,024, 1,024, and 512 tokens. Each buffer holds 16,384 positions.

    1. Compute the occupancy of a padded batch that allocates the maximum length to every row.
    2. Pack the examples by first-fit decreasing, placing each example, longest first, in the first buffer with room. Report the buffers, the packed occupancy, and the factor by which packing reduces allocated positions.
    3. For the buffer holding the 8,192-, 6,144-, and 2,048-token examples, compute the fraction of causal score entries (the lower triangle, diagonal included) that block-diagonal attention (equation 4) sets to \(-\infty\). Explain what that fraction means for a variable-length attention kernel, and why the mask, not the skipped work, is the correctness requirement.

  5. When on-policy labeling pays (Intermediate). Offline imitation of the repair agent reaches a disagreement rate of \(\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate=0.01\) on reference states over a horizon of \(\TurnHorizon=50\) decision steps. Three DAgger rounds would each collect 400 trajectories that run the full horizon and label all 50 visited states of each, and each label costs $0.05 to obtain and $0.25 to verify by a sandbox continuation. After aggregation, the retrained policy’s disagreement on its own states is \(\LearnerStateDisagreementRate=0.02\), and one deviation followed by reference recovery costs at most \(\DeviationDisadvantage=2\).

    1. Compute both bounds of theorem 16.1, their ratio, and the reference-state error offline imitation would need to match the learner-state bound.
    2. Compute the labeling cost of the three rounds, and the horizon at which the reference-state bound saturates at its cap.
    3. A remediation step for the remediation agent changes live configuration with no adequate reversal, so one deviation there can spoil the rest of the task. Recompute the learner-state bound with \(\DeviationDisadvantage=\TurnHorizon\) at the same \(\LearnerStateDisagreementRate\), and state whether further labeling or a runtime mechanism should carry that step’s reliability.

  6. Reading preference margins (Intermediate). Two checkpoints trained with equation 7 at \(\PreferenceScale=0.1\) are compared on one admitted repair pair. On the selected spans, the reference log-probability sums are \(-22\) for the preferred record and \(-24\) for the dispreferred one. Checkpoint A assigns \(-20\) and \(-25\); checkpoint B assigns \(-23\) and \(-30\). The sums exclude a shared 1,000-token prefix with identical conditioning, whose inclusion would add \(+0.5\) to each record’s score under either checkpoint.

    1. Compute each checkpoint’s scores \(\PreferredReferenceRelativeScore\) and \(\DispreferredReferenceRelativeScore\), margin \(\PreferenceMargin\), pair loss, saturation factor \(\SigmoidFunction(-\PreferenceMargin)\), and full gradient coefficient \(\PreferenceScale\SigmoidFunction(-\PreferenceMargin)\).
    2. Show that including the shared prefix leaves every quantity in part (a) unchanged.
    3. A team proposes promoting checkpoint B because its pair loss is lower. Critique the proposal from the scores, and name the measurement that decides between the checkpoints.

  7. Sizing an adapter run on a fixed memory budget (Intermediate). A hypothetical base model has 20 billion parameters. The run attaches rank-32 adapters to four matrices in each of 48 layers, and each adapted matrix has 6,144 input and 6,144 output coordinates. Use the chapter’s convention of 16 bytes of state per trainable parameter and 2 bytes per frozen parameter, with decimal GB. The accelerator offers 48 GB, of which 16 GB is reserved for activations and workspace.

    1. Compute the adapter parameter count, its fraction of the base, and the parameter state of full fine-tuning and of the adapter run. Decide whether either fits the remaining budget.
    2. Store the frozen base at 4 bits per parameter plus 0.5 bits per parameter of quantization metadata. Recompute the adapter run’s parameter state, decide whether it fits, and compute what doubling the rank would add.
    3. Five agent roles each need their own adaptation. With every weight stored at 2 bytes per parameter, compare five full-weight copies with one shared base plus five adapters, and state what the shared base does not provide between roles.

  8. Context distillation for the support agent (Advanced). The support agent runs short sessions under concurrency, so its binding constraint is per-turn input and response latency rather than the long observations that dominate the repair corpus. Each turn’s input carries a 3,000-token procedural checklist, a 1,500-token refund policy that changes weekly, and about 1,500 tokens of conversation and customer record. Sessions average four turns, and the deployment serves 2 million sessions per month. Input tokens cost $2 per million, and a cached prefix is billed at one tenth of that rate. Each redistillation, a training run plus the release evaluation, costs $15,000, and the checklist is revised once a month.

    1. Using table 1, decide which of the three input segments may be distilled into the weights and which must stay in context.
    2. Compute the monthly input tokens and spend that distilling the checklist removes, first with no prefix caching and then with the stable prefix always billed at the cached rate. Compute the net monthly saving after redistillation in each case, and the number of checklist revisions per month at which the uncached saving reaches zero.
    3. Decide whether to distill under each serving configuration. State what must be measured, rather than inferred from the token reduction, before claiming a latency gain, and contrast the constraint that decides this case with the one that governs masking for the repair agent.

  9. Critique a release proposal (Advanced). A team proposes promoting a fine-tuned repair policy on the following evidence. Its all-token training loss fell. On 500 closed-loop trials each, the baseline was accepted in 60 percent of trials at a mean attempt cost of $1.20, and the candidate in 66 percent at $1.50. On familiar interfaces, schema-following accuracy rose from 0.92 to 0.95. On a renamed-argument test set, the baseline scored an SFA of 0.90 with a PRE of 0.02, and the candidate an SFA of 0.81 with a PRE of 0.12.

    1. Compute \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) for each policy in the per-attempt form of equation 1.2, and the largest mean attempt cost at which the candidate would match the baseline.
    2. Using a normal approximation for independent trials, compute the standard error of the difference in acceptance rates and the difference measured in standard errors.
    3. Interpret the pattern in SFA and PRE, and redesign the release gate. Name what the decision should be now, which measurements must be added or repeated, and which training change to test first.
    4. Suppose the two arms in fact ran on the same 500 tasks. State what record the paired comparison of section 16.11 needs beyond the two acceptance rates, and explain how the count of discordant tasks could make the difference in part (b) more or less convincing than the independent-trials calculation suggests.

What’s Next: From admitted responses to reward-scored interaction
When a task offers an outcome check but no demonstrated response for each decision, the adaptation framework must evolve beyond supervised imitation and static comparisons. Every update in this chapter needed a demonstrated or compared response, so the version conflict that trained_policy_v1 never saw could be taught only by labeling that state or injecting it. Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards starts from that checkpoint and lets the repair agent’s acceptance check score fresh attempts on the states the policy reaches by itself, turning each verdict into a weight update. The check then stops being only a release gate and becomes the objective. That shift raises questions this chapter’s objectives never posed: whether the reward resists the shortcuts optimization will find, how one terminal verdict assigns credit across many turns, how rollout records stay faithful to the policy that sampled them, and what the rollout and verifier fleet costs. Containment and closed-loop release evidence apply as before.
